#6831. Cisco Intros Privileged Infrastructure Access for People and AI Agents

Cisco has introduced Privileged Infrastructure Access, an approach to infrastructure administration that gives people and AI agents temporary, task-specific permissions instead of leaving standing privileges available after their work is done.

In a recent blog post, Cisco's Matt Caulfield described a system that uses cryptographic identity to connect an administrator or agent to the infrastructure needed for a particular task. Access is governed by short-lived privileges, with session recording providing a record of the work performed.

"If there is no work being done, no privilege exists," Caulfield wrote. Cisco positions the offering as a way to bring identity controls directly into infrastructure operations while reducing dependence on static credentials, shared secrets, and broad access.

The announcement follows Cisco's previously announced Teleport partnership and its Infrastructure Identity concept, which connects the identity of a person or machine with the resources it can access, the actions it performs, and the permissions governing those actions.

Caulfield described a gap between the controls organizations apply to workforce applications and those used to administer network infrastructure. Companies have invested in single sign-on, multifactor authentication, and risk-based controls for application access, he wrote, but those protections often do not follow an engineer logging into a router or switch.

Instead, infrastructure access may depend on long-lived credentials, shared accounts, or separate policy systems. According to Cisco, that separation makes it harder to enforce least privilege and establish which person or system performed a particular action.

The company illustrates the problem with an administrator updating hundreds of switches during a maintenance window. If some updates fail, the administrator needs to troubleshoot before the window closes. The permissions available to handle that work, however, may cover more infrastructure and remain active longer than the task requires.

Traditional privileged access management can reduce risk through credential vaulting and rotation, Caulfield wrote. But he argued that operational friction can undermine those controls when teams create exceptions to complete urgent work. Those exceptions can leave credentials and privileges usable after the immediate need has passed.

Cisco's stated objective is to give infrastructure teams a consistent route to the devices they manage while providing security teams with policy enforcement, temporary permissions, and a record of activity. The company describes operational convenience as part of the design, rather than treating access governance solely as a credential-management problem.

Privileged Infrastructure Access makes access an on-demand action, according to the announcement. Cryptographic identity establishes the actor, and the resulting connection provides only the privileges needed to complete the task. Those privileges expire when the task concludes.

For customers using Cisco Identity Services Engine (ISE) and Terminal Access Controller Access-Control System Plus (TACACS+), the offering can draw on existing policy and device context to govern access to Cisco routers and switches. Cisco says customers can extend the same access model to other infrastructure, including servers, Kubernetes clusters, and cloud resources.

Video game designers spend an enormous amount of time studying what keeps people engaged when something becomes difficult. They have become very good at helping people persist. Schools should pay attention.

OpenAI has introduced GPT-6 Astra, its most capable broadly deployed model and the first system the company says has reached the "Critical" cybersecurity capability threshold under its Preparedness Framework.

SMART Technologies has introduced SoundFlow, a plug-and-play broadcasting system that allows classroom audio content to flow directly from a SMART Board display to students' compatible hearing aids, earbuds, headphones, personal listening devices, or speakers.

CrowdStrike's latest Threat Hunting Report traces a multiyear shift from conventional intrusions toward attacks that exploit trusted identities, cloud services, AI systems, and software dependencies.

#6830. This Brand-New Illinois Farmhouse Was Designed to Look Hundreds of Years Old

The team at Buccellato Design and Architecture focused on incorporating heritage details and timeworn furnishings.

For Aimee and Kevin Buccellato, the best compliment they could receive about a project isn’t, “Wow, it looks so different in here.” It’s that a home looks like they haven’t changed a thing.

On a country road in Momence, Illinois, a beloved farmhouse stands as a timeworn landmark for the surrounding agricultural community. For the homeowners—a retired couple returning to their Midwestern roots—the property was as much a repository of memories as it was a place to create new ones. “Having grown up nearby, the property evoked a strong sense of memory and place,” says Aimee, who founded Buccellato Design and Architecture alongside her husband, Kevin. “At the outset of the project, and throughout, our clients wanted the new house to feel like it had always been there, so much so that perhaps the neighbors could be led to believe that the original house remained intact, yet amended by skillful renovation and discreet additions.”

It was an architectural sleight of hand rooted in a distinctly Midwestern philosophy: “Be good to the neighbors.” Alongside the Buccellatos, the couple approached the 6,000-square-foot rebuild with the restraint of preservationists and the imagination of storytellers. “Great care was taken to instill the home and outbuildings with a sense of history and rootedness, creating a home that feels as though it’s been part of this bucolic setting for centuries,” says Kevin of the project, which also included the restoration of the property’s original 19th-century barn and corn crib. “We drew inspiration from traditional farm complexes, where homesteads were added slowly over time, reconnecting the site with its Folk-Victorian-Farm-House and agricultural building roots.”

Inside, the narrative continues through antique white oak floors, salvaged beams, heritage florals, and rooms that borrow their palettes from the landscape. In the kitchen, a wall of windows replaces upper cabinetry, framing sunrise over the barn and walnut trees. The glass porch offers sweeping views of the Kankakee River, while a tucked-away speakeasy recalls the original farmhouse’s stone foundation.

For Kevin and Aimee, the guiding principle was a new construction home that felt “sensitive, not wasteful, polite, not ostentatious,” an exercise in knowing when architecture and interiors should defer to their surroundings. The result is a house with an invented past and a very real sense of belonging that does the Midwest—and the neighbors—proud.

FAST FACTSDesigner: Buccellato Design and ArchitectureStylist: Cate RaganLocation: Momence, IllinoisThe Space: A four-bedroom, four-and-a-half-bathroom home across 6,000 square feet

The entryway offers a fitting introduction to the home'’s carefully constructed sense of history, replete with nickel gap paneling, fumed and waxed antique white oak floors, and a light-filled staircase. Even the furnishings—including a vintage Persian rug and antique Jacobean gateleg table—suggest a collection acquired over generations.

Benjamin Moore’s Nelson Blue sets a serene backdrop for the sitting room, where traditional silhouettes and understated patterns make an easy pairing. “This room was designed to honor the history of this property and embody the love that our clients hold for this special place,” adds Aimee.

A tufted mohair sofa, realized in a rich umber, lends a sense of history, while an iconic feather lamp by Bunny Williams offers a reminder that even the most classic rooms benefit from a little irreverence.

In the kitchen, the Buccellatos traded upper cabinets for an expansive wall of windows overlooking the farmland, relocating the practical necessities to an adjacent dish room and larder. Salvaged white oak beams and barnboards overhead reinforce the home’s agricultural roots, while furniture-like cabinetry and leathered black quartzite countertops strike a timeless note.

With natural light pouring in from three sides, the dining room is what the Buccellatos describe as “peaceful, soft, and ethereal,” equally suited to both morning coffee and candlelit dinners. A dreamy mural wallpaper by Susan Harter references the property’s location along the Kankakee River, while formal seating lends a sense of occasion without sacrificing the home’s easygoing country charm.

Nestled among mature trees, the primary bedroom feels almost suspended in the surrounding landscape. The Buccellatos leaned into that sense of serenity with the palette. “It’s a dreamlike mix of earthy neutrals and pale blues that paints a serene backdrop for elegant details, vintage furnishings, and a delicate mix of subtle pattern,” adds Kevin.

A French country canopy bed, dressed in tailored Schumacher linen, takes center stage, while vintage-inspired furnishings and subtle pattern keep the room from feeling overly precious. An adjacent sitting room offers a place to read the morning paper or unwind with a cup of tea in the evenings.

In the vestibule, a little pattern goes a long way. A climbing floral from Schumacher in inky charcoal makes a charming counterpoint to the bedroom’s hushed palette, while a checked window bench is the ideal spot to read, giving the passageway purpose.

Pale blue walls and creamy millwork lend the primary bathroom an old-fashioned prettiness. A custom vanity introduces a tailored architectural element, while a Cowtan & Tout floral Roman shade softens the room’s more utilitarian details.

Designed as a playful respite for guests and future grandchildren, this bedroom plays off pattern and primary hues. “The red painted woodwork feels unexpected and cheerful against the blue-and-white wallpaper, striped rug, and patterned bedding,” says Aimee.

“The brick-clad main block echoes the footprint of the original farmhouse on this site, yet the story we wrote for the house begins with the folk Victorian wing, inspired by L-shaped farmhouses of that period,” says Kevin. “In our modern narrative, the clapboard-sided wing came first, followed by the brick-clad ’addition,’ which takes inspiration from the later Colonial Revival period in its scale and sophistication of detailing.”

Founded by Aimee and Kevin Buccellato, Buccellato Design & Architecture is a practice marked by twenty-plus years of close collaboration, in architectural practice and in life. With projects on both coasts and throughout the country, the firm’s award-winning work reveals a dedication to craft, artistry, and detail. Achieved in partnership with clients, their projects reflect a purposeful sense of rootedness in the landscape and abiding devotion to beauty and the comfort of home.

#6829. Colin Jost Takes Gift-Giving Seriously

The Weekend Update co-host and Kay campaign star shares his approach to holiday shopping.

Every item on this page was chosen by an ELLE editor. We may earn commission on some of the items you choose to buy.

Finding the perfect holiday gift isn’t easy. Between the pressure to get it right, the endless wish lists, and the people who insist they don’t want anything, shopping for loved ones can feel like an impossible mission. Even Colin Jost, who has spent years navigating the pressures of live television, admits that the process is incredibly stressful.

This festive season, the Weekend Update co-host is putting his comedic talents to work with Kay by starring in the jeweler’s latest holiday campaign, “Love All In.” The cheeky ad follows Jost on an increasingly ridiculous quest to find the perfect present, poking fun at the lengths we’ll go to for the people closest to us. It’s a fitting partnership for someone who knows a thing or two about making people laugh but also takes gifting pretty seriously.

“I think I’m a good gift giver,” Jost tells ELLE. “Giving gifts is stressful because I do want it to be a meaningful gift. I hate the idea of just buying something because you have to give someone something.”

For Jost, the secret to getting it right has less to do with how much you spend and more to do with how much thought goes into it. “At every kind of price point for any gift, there [are] thoughtful and unthoughtful versions,” he says. “Something can be really expensive but not thoughtful.”

It’s a sentiment that aligns with Kay’s latest campaign, which encourages shoppers to go “all in” when expressing their love. And while Jost might not be the first person you’d think to turn to for jewelry advice, he’s developed an appreciation for it over the years, thanks to his very chic wife, Scarlett Johansson.

“Until five years ago, I didn’t own a watch,” he admits. That changed when the actress gifted him his first one, introducing him to an entirely new world of accessories. As for whether she’s influenced his personal style? “In that it’s exclusively managed by her, yes, I would say she’s influenced it.”

Despite his Staten Island roots, Jost’s jewelry preferences are surprisingly understated. “I don’t really wear a ton of jewelry, but I also am from Staten Island, so I do have to have certain pieces, or else they won’t let me back over the Verrazano Bridge,” he jokes.

While browsing Kay’s assortment, he found himself drawn to simple gold pieces, including a ring, bracelet, and necklace. “They were really nice, but also they weren’t gaudy or anything,” he says. His preference for gold also comes with some rather unconventional reasoning: “If we get into a post-apocalyptic situation, it feels like it’s better to melt down for trading.”

When it comes to shopping for the people closest to him, Jost admits that both Johansson and his Weekend Update collaborator Michael Che are difficult to buy things for, though he found a creative solution for the latter. “I got Che a really large and heavy, realistic statue of a velociraptor,” he recalls. “It was so heavy that he literally can’t get rid of it.” The real expense wasn’t the dinosaur itself. “The main cost was shipping,” he says. “It was a huge eyesore, and it’s prohibitively expensive for him to have removed from his property.”

The hardest person to gift on Jost’s list? His father, who insists he doesn’t need anything. “I think he actually means it, but it’s so unhelpful,” Jost says. After years of nostalgic gifts inspired by his dad’s past, he’s running out of ideas. “I’ve got to figure out something that breaks through his defenses.”

In terms of gifting etiquette, Jost has a few rules of his own. For starters, he’s big on surprises. While he understands the appeal of a wish list for children, he thinks adults should leave a little room for the unexpected. “For adults, it’s got to be ‘surprise me,’” he says. “The surprise is half the fun, three-quarters of the fun.” Regifting, on the other hand, comes with a few conditions. He’s open to the idea, but only if the gift is a perfect fit for someone and there’s full disclosure.

But perhaps the most surprising revelation comes when I ask what’s on his own holiday wish list. After spending much of our conversation discussing what to buy everyone else, Jost seems relieved to finally be on the receiving end. “Oh, thank you! Finally, someone asked me. What do I want?”

His answer? Nothing material. “At this point in life, I mostly want experiences,” he says. Whether it’s a trip or simply someone taking the initiative to organize an outing, he’s more interested in making memories than collecting more things. “Even if it’s just the plan of, ‘Let’s go do this at this time,’ I think that’s a great gift.” Or, when in doubt, a velociraptor.

#6828. Are ‘Love Is Blind’ Weddings Legally Binding? Everything We Know About the Wedding and Divorce Rules

I need to know just how “married” these couples really are....

Welp, another batch of strangers just had elaborate wedding ceremonies on reality TV! Love Is Blind Season 11 dropped its finale, and we found out which couples got married. This year, John and Sarah ended up saying “I do” at the altar, but were these weddings actually legally binding, or did Netflix pull a Let’s Marry Harry and leave the paperwork out of it? About that...

They most certainly are. The couples are married by real officiants, and real paperwork is filed. Meaning, if things don’t work out they have to go through divorce proceedings just like any other couple. As creator Chris Coelen told Bustle in 2020, "If they choose to get married, yes, it's a legally binding marriage."

Former cast member Lauren Speed also told Refinery29 that she and Cameron Hamilton “definitely did” get married, and that was the “part that was so scary for me. This is a real legally binding marriage. This is not for TV. This is our life."

In fact, the legitimacy of the Love Is Blind weddings is one of the ways the show gets spoiled. TMZ dug into marriage records and spoiled several weddings back in 2020, and fans on Reddit have also been known to sleuth.

According to The Tab, the same day as the wedding. The outlet reports that “it’s all done on the same day, often with a judge or ordained officiant present.”

Couples reportedly have to wait until 11 months after their wedding so that the show isn’t spoiled for viewers. As for who foots the bill, The Tab reports that production pays for up to $5,000 per couple for filings and mediation.

#6826. 4 Weekend Decluttering Habits That Make Small Spaces Feel Bigger

A few smart systems can keep your counters, closets, and junk drawers in check.

The easiest clutter to manage is clutter that never has a chance to form. And in small spaces like galley kitchens and studio apartments, mess piles up naturally on any flat surface. Keeping it under control is less about “magic bullet” tips and more about systems you can actually keep up over time. So when we asked the professional organizers about how to declutter small spaces, the answer was in mindset and habits. If you can limit what comes into your space in the first place, you can better control its organization.

“I could say, ‘Grab a garbage bag and pick up all of the detritus left in the wake of a busy life and then put everything else where it belongs.’ That’s obvious,” Gayle M. Gruenberg of Let’s Get Organized says. “The hard part is actually DOING it. It requires motivation, presence of mind, initiative, and the necessary tools.”

Below, find the pro-approved habits that make a difference when you have more stuff than your space can hold. You can start these routines in just one weekend, and it’s far more attainable than tackling the massive pile in the garage.

Everything needs a home in your house. That’s the power of a well-placed “drop zone.” Even in small spaces, a catchall tray, bowl, or mini basket belongs at entryways or places of transition. A hook for your keys and bags when you come through the door, a bin for loose throw blankets, or a basket for remote controls on the mantel.

Throughout the day, it can take quite a bit of mental load to always put everything back when you use it. That’s where Cathy Orr, cofounder of The Uncluttered Life, recommends having a “collection basket.” It’s just one catchall container, where you can drop miscellaneous misplaced items that you pick up throughout the day. At the end of the night, take about 10 minutes to go through the collection basket and return everything to where it belongs. It’s just one small habit, rather than something to keep up with all day.

Impulse shopping lends itself to clutter very easily. The “One In, One Out” rule can curb this. It works like this: for every new thing you want to bring into your home, one thing has to go. Greenhalgh suggests checking your closet, fridge, and pantry before you shop—going through your back stock will save you both space and money.

“In small spaces, items accumulate very quickly,” Macie Kreutzer, founder of For the Love of Simple, says. “Before shopping for new items (home goods, clothing) clear out at least one item from this category. Don't bring in more than your space can physically contain.”

Once you have everything you’re ready to get rid of, make the donation process as seamless as possible. Fill your donation bags and boxes in the hallway or in the car. This encourages you to actually finish getting rid of the clutter, rather than letting them collect dust in the garage.

“Nothing gets to wander back inside just because it once lived there,” Carroll Cartwright, professional organizer and owner of Neatly & Co., says. “And this is why I love moving everything out of the space and into the hall or a separate area first. The things you decide not to keep are already halfway out the door. Keep them moving.”

It is so tempting to take advantage of the freebies in life. But this is how linen closets and kitchen drawers get quickly overrun. Unless there’s a specific and immediate need for them, you’re better off getting rid of them.

“They are usually cheap and break easily anyway,” Diane Greenhalgh, owner of Tiny to the Max, says. “That includes airplane and hotel freebies, special event or conference swag, and extra takeout plastic utensils and condiments. Your junk drawer will thank you.”

If you already have a drawer overflowing with freebies, it’s time to start culling and evaluating. You might part with two or 10 things, or you might choose to keep it all. “The point is that you’ve looked at every single item and made a decision about it, instead of continuing to store things simply because they were already there,” Cartwright says. “More likely than not, you’ll find something that makes you say, ‘Why on earth do I still have this?’”

The advice your grandma gave you was spot-on: It’s easier if you wash the dishes as you cook. That same principle applies to keeping your small space tidy. Mess feels larger with less square footage to work with, so once you’re finished with something, put it away.

“Put away your makeup after you've applied it in the morning, stack dishes in the dishwasher and wipe down surfaces after each meal, and tuck away work or homework into a drawer once you're done for the evening,” Greenhalgh says.

For the days you can’t clean as you go, Cartwright suggests spending 10 to 15 minutes a day “closing your circles.” It’s what helps your home stay clean over time, instead of just for a few weeks. This period is when you put the scissors back in the drawer or hang up your jacket.

“Most clutter is just a collection of unfinished tasks,” Cartwright says. “This small commitment allows you to wake up to order instead of yesterday’s chaos, and that is a much better way to start the day!”

#6824. Where Is Joran van der Sloot Now? The Truth Behind Netflix’s ‘Taken: Natalee Holloway’

The new documentary revisits the 2005 disappearance that gripped the world.

Taken: Natalee Holloway is a new three-part Netflix documentary about the disappearance of 18-year-old Natalee Holloway, who vanished during a trip to Aruba in 2005. The series revisits the case through the eyes of her mother, Beth, who has never spoken in such detail about her ordeal before, along with the people who spent years searching for answers.

One question has followed the case for more than two decades: What really happened to Natalee, and what became of Joran van der Sloot, the man who admitted killing her but was never actually punished for it?

Joran van der Sloot is a Dutch man who was 17 when Natalee Holloway disappeared during what should have been a celebratory holiday with friends; he was one of the last people known to have seen her.

Natalee had traveled from Alabama to Aruba with classmates to celebrate their high school graduation, and was due to fly home on May 30, 2005. She was last seen leaving a bar in Oranjestad with van der Sloot and two other young men, brothers Deepak and Satish Kalpoe. All three were questioned and arrested during the investigation, but none was charged in connection with her disappearance at the time.

Natalee’s body has never been found, but van der Sloot has confessed to her murder.

Yes. In 2023, van der Sloot admitted that he killed Natalee in Aruba. According to a transcript of his account, he said they were walking on a beach when she rejected his advances. He said he kicked her and hit her with a cinder block, then pushed her body into the sea.

The confession came as part of a U.S. case against van der Sloot. He had tried to extort money from Natalee's mother, Beth Holloway, by promising to reveal the location of her daughter’s remains and reveal to her what had happened.

He pleaded guilty to extortion and wire fraud and was sentenced to 20 years in prison.

However, van der Sloot was never convicted of Natalee’s murder. The U.S. case was about extortion and wire fraud, and he has not been charged with her killing. So although he confessed, it is more accurate to say he admitted to killing Natalee than to describe him as a convicted murderer (at least in the case of Natalee).

Van der Sloot is in prison in Peru, where he is serving a 28-year sentence for the 2010 murder of Peruvian student Stephany Flores Ramírez. His U.S. sentence runs at the same time as his Peruvian sentence.

As of August 2026, he was reported to be held at Challapalca prison in southern Peru. He had asked to be moved, alleging threats from other inmates; reports said a judge still needed to rule on his request.

Stephany Flores Ramírez was a 21-year-old Peruvian business student. In May 2010, she was found dead in a hotel room in Lima, after checking in with Joran van der Sloot.

Van der Sloot was arrested in Chile and taken back to Peru. He initially confessed to killing Stephany, but later tried to withdraw his confession—however, a court ruled that it could be used as evidence.

In January 2012, he was sentenced to 28 years in prison for her murder and ordered to pay compensation to Stephany’s family.

The timing was especially eerie, as it is believed that Stephany was killed on the fifth anniversary of Natalee's disappearance. Although her case was separate, van der Sloot's arrest put him back in the spotlight and brought fresh attention to the questions that still surrounded Natalee's disappearance.

In 2023, van der Sloot admitted to killing her too, although he has never been convicted of Natalee's murder.

#6822. Survey Finds 84% of Organizations Reported at Least One AI-Related Incident in the Past Year

Companies are running into problems as they give artificial intelligence more control over critical business applications, with 84% reporting at least one AI-related incident in the past year, according to a new survey from identity security firm Delinea.

The company's 2026 Application Access Governance report found that some organizations have experienced incorrect financial transactions and AI actions they couldn't trace or explain. The findings raise concerns about whether enterprises have enough oversight of AI systems that can make decisions and take actions on their own

The findings are based on an independent survey of 1,000 senior finance and finance technology leaders in the United States and United Kingdom. The AI-specific analysis included 879 respondents whose organizations use the technology in critical business applications.

The report found that 35% of surveyed organizations have deployed AI agents capable of taking actions within business applications. However, only 11% of organizations using AI said their governance and security controls were keeping pace with deployment.

Delinea also found gaps in centralized visibility into agent deployment and permissions, including who owns agents and who approved their use.

Some respondents reported segregation-of-duties conflicts involving AI, while others identified agents that continued operating after the employees who deployed them had left the organization.

The research also highlighted gaps in monitoring access across business systems. Only 46% of respondents said they continuously monitor and manage cumulative access risks across applications.

Those gaps can make it difficult to identify segregation-of-duties conflicts, in which a person or agent has permissions across systems that should be kept separate.

Despite those issues, 95% of respondents said they were confident their access controls would withstand scrutiny in an external audit.

However, 26% reported access-related audit findings that had recurred in consecutive years, while 25% said some findings had remained unresolved for three or more years.

Only 22% of organizations had a single named owner for each application, with ownership tracked in one system of record.

Meanwhile, 60% reported an increase over the past two years in the number of critical applications operating outside the scope of access governance or Sarbanes-Oxley Act compliance.

The findings highlight the oversight challenges associated with giving AI agents authority to act within business systems, particularly when organizations cannot consistently identify who owns an agent or trace its actions.

Frank Vukovits, chief security scientist at Delinea, said organizations remain responsible for the actions AI agents take within their systems.

"When an agent can approve a transaction or change permissions, the business owns the outcome. You need to know what every agent can do, who owns it, and who approved it, before an auditor asks or your financials are misstated," Vukovits said.

For the full report, go to the Delinea site.

Microsoft has shut down EvilTokens, a cybercrime service that used AI to help attackers break into e-mail accounts, study their victims, and build convincing financial scams.

Leaders in the artificial intelligence industry have warned for years that increasingly capable AI systems could eventually become dangerous, all the while continuing to race to build them. Now, their sentiments have changed.

According to a survey by data and AI services provider Further, enterprises are reporting benefits from artificial intelligence even as their data preparation and oversight processes remain incomplete.

Unauthorized artificial intelligence tools and agents are creating security risks for organizations as AI adoption increases, according to cybersecurity company Sophos.

#6821. Irish Designers Share Their 7 Coziest Style Secrets That We Should All Be Stealing

Here’s how to embrace that “Green Wave” with your interiors.

Over the past few years, Americans have become notably charmed by Irish culture—even the L.A. Times coined it a “Green Wave.” From splitting the “G” when drinking a pint of Guinness and bragging about it on social media to spotlighting Irish celebrities like author Sally Rooney, musician Hozier, and actress Saoirse Ronan, we as a society—myself very much included—have become a bit obsessed with the Emerald Isle, and I think it’s high time we let this Green Wave roll into the interior design scene.

If you’ve ever had the privilege to visit a home in the Emerald Isle, you’ll know that Irish spaces are warm, welcoming, and lived-in, with many of the design elements specifically used to prioritize comfort and community, something American homeowners have begun adopting. “Irish homes have traditionally needed to be comfortable, functional, and adaptable to a climate that can be cold, damp, and unpredictable,” Galway-based designer Niamh McKenna tells House Beautiful. “Traditionally, our homes are also much smaller, so beautiful rooms must also work for everyday life.”

However, it’s not just the environment which influences their interiors. Keep reading to find out the quintessentially Irish design details that local designers love to celebrate and believe American homes should borrow.

During the Georgian era, which spanned from the early 1700s to the early 1800s, Ireland experienced a population boom. During this time, Dublin also underwent a sizable redevelopment when it comes to architecture and urban infrastructure, according to History Ireland. It’s these elegant details that designer Julianne Kelly loves so much and thinks others should adopt.

“There is so much history in these houses and the proportions are beautiful: high ceilings, ornate cornicing, original sash windows, fireplaces, and rich paneled doors,” Kelly says. “These details give a room a sense of history and permanence.”

The Merrion Square area in Dublin, she adds, is a great place to walk around to view some of these rich elements in person, specifically the iconic painted Georgian doors.

Once you leave the streets of Georgian Dublin, you’ll find the sprawling countryside with “a beautiful, varied landscape that is impossible to ignore when designing the interiors for a house,” Kelly explains. She uses many natural elements found just outside the window in her designs, like greenery, stone, timber, and natural fibers like wool and linen that feel authentic to the country and only improve with age. Colors inspired by the scenes outside help the interior feel connected to the earth as well.

The textiles specifically add much-needed warmth and coziness to a room. “I love these as they add personality, a sense of calm, and an old worldliness that doesn’t look over manufactured or brand-spanking new!”

“The fireplace has traditionally been at the heart of the Irish home, and I think that idea is still incredibly relevant,” Kelly says. Ornate or simple, stone or tiled, wood-burning or gas—the details of the fireplace don’t really matter, as long as it serves as a natural focal point and the furniture is arranged around it.

McKenna adds that centering the hearth rather than the TV promotes conversation and connection. “It’s a reminder that a room can be designed around how people gather and interact,” she explains. The energy and warmth of a fireplace, even as modern homes move away from open fires, brings comfort and beauty to any room, and that’s something worth preserving.

Step into an Irish person’s home, and you’ll likely find a lot of old mixed in with the new. “Irish people love to hold onto their items,” Kelly says. “I’m not sure if it’s due to our famine history, but on most projects I work on, people have sentimental items that I have to work with.”

The designer will always try to work with these existing pieces to come up with some sort of eclectic mix of modern and vintage. She loves how unique and personal these homes turn out to be; they tell a great story. “And the Irish love a good story,” she adds.

In Ireland’s rainy climate, a home’s interiors—especially practical, hardworking spaces—need to feel both beautiful and comfortable.

“Bathrooms and kitchens are among the hardest-working parts of our homes, and we see it important to give the most functional part of our homes the same thought and attention as our living spaces,” McKenna explains. “We love to connect the indoors to the outdoors in these spaces, by using natural materials, earthy colors, and organic textures to create an interior that is as beautiful to experience as it is practical to use.”

Much of the residential architecture in Ireland is designed to work with the chilly, wet climate. “Traditional Irish architecture features deep window reveals, thick walls, and smaller windows than what might be seen elsewhere,” McKenna explains.

These choices could easily make a home feel dark and cold, but the designer says that when oriented correctly to the outside light, these elements only add to a cozy, warm environment in the winter and a cool place for relief in the summer. “I love that these characteristics also create a depth, shadow, and texture that are difficult to replicate with decorative finishes alone,” she adds.

Similarly to the architecture, the interiors are decorated to account for the damp climate, as well. “We make comfort a priority rather than a finishing touch, by layering furnishings such as window treatments, tactile rugs and cushions, and blankets,” McKenna says. Even if someone were to decorate their home rather minimally, the layers of tactile fabrics found in Irish homes still ensure the space feels inviting and lived-in.

#6817. 5 Common Mistakes That Are Making Your Vintage Decor Look All Wrong—and How Designers Get It Right

Experts say the best rooms mix eras, styles, and stories.

Every item on this page was hand-picked by a House Beautiful editor. We may earn commission on some of the items you choose to buy.

Pictured above: This space, designed by Sarah West, strikes the right balance of vintage and contemporary decor, proving that a mix is always better than sticking to one design era.

It’s not just the Instagram algorithm playing tricks on you: Everyone really is looking to antique and vintage decor. Homeowners are growing tired of rooms that look as though everything was bought from a single brand in one fell swoop. We’re now at a point where a collected, curated, personalized feel has replaced quiet luxury.

“I think it’s important to add at least some kind of antique or vintage piece in every room,” says Sarah West, founder of Sarah West & Associates and a designer for over 20 years. “Even if you’re doing a more modern or transitional room, a sense of history adds weight to a space.”

It’s not necessarily as easy as plopping one antique into a room and calling it a day, however, and that can feel intimidating. Even once you learn to shop for antiques and vintage like a pro, what do you actually do with those finds when you’re home?

While the designers we spoke with are quick to point out there really are no strict rules here—it’s about your style and what you love—there are a few key mistakes that pop up most often when people start styling with antique and vintage decor. These designers have the scoop on how to avoid those missteps and make your treasures, and your entire room, shine.

Committing to a massive piece of furniture or tasking yourself with overhauling an entire room with antique or vintage pieces can get overwhelming fast. You may make decisions you don’t love down the line.

If you decide you’re going to decorate a whole room in a limited amount of time, and you’re set on sourcing antique or vintage items, you’ll likely end up settling for things just because you need them, says Nancy Cavaliere, a New York–based interior decorator, stylist, and vintage collector. Go slow and start small.

“I’d rather live with an empty corner while I wait for the perfect piece than fill it with something I don’t love,” she notes. “The most interesting homes evolve over time. I think a room should tell the story of the person living in it, not the story of one shopping trip.”

Ease your way into decorating with vintage and antiques by starting small. Think one piece of art, one mirror, one vase.

“Don’t overthink it,” West advises. “Take a small piece, try it out in the room for a couple of days, see how it feels.” Do you like the piece in that spot? Does it work with other elements of the room? Does it inspire other styling choices for the space? Try moving it around and interacting with it to find its perfect positioning and see where that takes you.

As you start out getting a feel for that smaller piece, remember that just because it’s unique doesn’t mean it has to be the focal point of the whole room.

“Don’t treat vintage pieces like they have to be the star of the room simply because they’re old,” says Cavaliere. “Not every antique needs a pedestal. Sometimes the most beautiful thing you can do is let an extraordinary piece sit comfortably alongside something completely ordinary.”

Fully incorporating vintage or antique pieces into a space imbues the room with that richness and intrigue.

“These pieces give feelings to a room,” West says. “If you put something in a corner, as you transition throughout the room, it gives this element of surprise.”

This includes creating high-low mixes, says Cavaliere, who points out, “a $20 flea market find can look incredible next to an expensive designer piece. It’s all about how you put things together.”

In fact, that mix is something to strive for, says Alex Bass, founder of Salon 21, Art Advisory & Interior Design Firm.

“I love a contrast, like a room with modern architecture that leans heavily into antiques or vice versa. If you want to incorporate pieces from Pottery Barn, CB2, that’s fine, but there are also so many affordable vintage finds. And something like an antique light fixture can make all of these pieces work.”

Speaking of contrasts, all of our designer sources say a very common misconception they encounter is that you can only decorate a room with pieces from the same era.

“Just because you have a Victorian chair doesn’t mean you need a Victorian living room,” Cavaliere says. “My own home is a mix of different periods, styles, colors, and patterns, and that's what gives it personality. I love the tension between pieces that seemingly have nothing in common. And it's fun!”

Bass cites the example of Art Deco being popular right now, while articles are claiming midcentury modern is “out.” She encourages people to think of pieces less rigidly, assuming they have to commit fully to one style, or toss another style because someone declared a new one the next big thing.

“These are not ‘trends,’ these are design periods that already existed,” she explains. “It’s about having the eye to pull pieces from different periods that speak to you and make sense in your room.” You can mix Art Deco and midcentury modern, for instance, by using elements characteristic of them both, like marble, warm woods, mixed metals, and bold color pops.

There can be a fear with vintage and antique pieces that changing anything “ruins” them. But unless it’s a significantly valuable piece you’re investing in or something you plan on having appraised, Bass says, embrace the ability to make special pieces work for your space.

“Don’t be afraid to reupholster,” she says—and not just if the fabric is in poor shape. “If I buy a piece and the structure is beautiful but I want to add a contemporary fabric and print, that can make for a fun contrast with the frame.”

For example, Bass fell in love with a modular midcentury sectional, but its fabric didn’t quite complement the space she was designing, so she chose a contemporary fabric that worked with its aesthetic.

This, Cavaliere points out, frees you up from obsessing over condition when shopping for pieces. If something has great bones but its fabric or finish has seen better days—and if you love it—don’t pass it up. Remember you can reupholster, strip and stain or paint, and repair.

Just factor the cost of those fixes into the price of the item to help make your decision. Especially because, as Bass advises, it’s better to consult a professional unless you’re well-versed in certain projects. Stripping and staining a wood piece is fairly easy, but when it comes to reupholstering and other repairs, you’ll likely actually save money by outsourcing to a pro rather than potentially damaging the piece yourself.

Similarly, it’s typical for people to feel they can’t actually make use of vintage and antique pieces, because they think they’re too precious. But especially considering how well-made many older pieces are, they’re begging to be put to work. Again, it’s actually incorporating them into your everyday life that gives your room and entire home that sense of personalization, curation, and storytelling.

“One hundred years ago, people were using their silver,” West says. “In my kitchen, you’re eating with hotel silver.”

This includes getting creative with how you use antique and vintage pieces—don’t feel bound by what something is “supposed to” be for.

“One of my favorite tricks is turning vintage drapes and valances into bed canopies,” Cavaliere says. “I did this in my daughter’s room and I absolutely love it.” Cavaliere also finds unexpected uses for small vessels and decorative objects: a creamer and sugar set in the bathroom to hold Q-tips and cotton balls; dishes, trays, and crystal bowls to sort accessories in her closet.

“A vintage china cabinet doesn't have to live in a dining room just because it was designed to hold dishes,” she says. “One of my favorite tricks is using china cabinets to display and store shoes and handbags in a dressing room or closet. Why spend a ton of money on custom cabinetry when you can repurpose a beautiful vintage piece that has so much more character?”

West agrees. “Purchase an armoire, flip the doors, add mirrors, and make it a bar,” West suggests. “Find an old door knocker you love? Hang a towel off of it. Use a tablecloth to make a pillow. Tell a story.”

#6816. The 2026 Esquire Cologne Awards

Every product was carefully curated by an Esquire editor. We may earn a commission from these links. Here’s how we test products and why you should trust us.

Whether you’re looking to make a big impression on a special occasion or just searching for a great everyday scent, these winners have you covered.

There’s never been a better time to be a guy who wants to smell good. The men’s fragrance market is booming, the scents are bolder than ever, and you can shop for pretty much anything you could possibly imagine—from long-lasting extracts for a big night out to easy-to-wear options that’ll fit right in at the office—with ease.

The only problem with all that abundance is the project of narrowing things down to a manageable number of scents that might work for you. Which is where we come in. This year, the team here at Esquire decided to go big for our annual Cologne Awards. Our fragrance experts called in hundreds of new releases over the course of the year, narrowing them down through extensive testing and internal debate before each creating their own short list of winners.

That’s when things got really interesting. Round by round, the Esquire Cologne Awards committee met to discuss potential winners, spritzing and testing again and again before finally producing the list you see here. These are the 40 best scents of the year. You might not connect with every one of them—but if you can’t find something you love here, well, good luck out there.

This has become one of our daily wears—and for good reason. It’s going to please pretty much anyone. Spicy and aromatic, this cologne is vanilla and amber forward with discernible notes of Iris germanica and lavender giving it a more floral edge. The result is sensual and soft but never unmasculine, thanks to the aromatic freshness. Plus, it comes packaged in a giant flask. There’s nothing manlier than having a giant flask (literally) of cologne behind your bathroom mirror.

Leather lovers, this one is for you. Tom Ford’s newest Ombré Leather Reserve blends notes of earthy, black leather with silver juniper and sandalwood, creating a balanced, rooted, yet refreshing take on Ford’s classic Ombré Leather eau de parfum. Inspired by the great American West and the mountains and deserts that constitute it, this scent is a perfect balance between refinement and rugged masculinity.

If you were around in the ’90s and spent some time at the mall, you probably remember Gap Dream. It was the most iconic scent of the original lineup, which was discontinued in the early aughts. But Gap has given the people what they want: a relaunch of its beloved fragrance line. Dream remains the hero of the collection, fresh and clean like a brand-new white T-shirt, with bright citrus, soft florals, and botanical musk.

Noble but knows how to fuck. A raw oud with fresh sensuality. The peach is jam-like, adding a softness to the suede accord. Velvet Mood is a leather fragrance with added warmth, texture, and juicy sweetness. (It recalls the intimate, sexual encounter in Call Me by Your Name. The one with the peach. You know it.) It’s captivating and aura-maxxing, not a blind buy for most. But we’re still on the side of biting the peach.

This cologne has one job, and that job is beach. It’s salty, woody, and as fresh as anything else we smelled this year. From the blond driftwood to the beach rose, coconut water, and sea salt, all the notes are reminiscent of tropical paradise. It may not be summer anymore, but the perfect summer scent has landed. Now that it’s in your possession, you can turn any day into a beach day.

Though it is totally within the Ranger Station wheelhouse of western-inspired cowpoke smells, it’s a new earthy floral. The easy choice would have been to mix sandalwood and ambergris with whiskey and call it another saloon smell. Instead the Ranger Station team gave it an iris root heart—soft and floral without losing its edge. It’s not betting the farm and drinking away the loss. It’s betting the farm, losing it, and stepping out those double swing doors to see a sunrise and smell a new dewy and green morning. You lost it all, but that was yesterday. Keep moving forward and the next win can’t be too far down the road.

It took four years to get right and was, according to the company, the last thing on which Giorgio Armani put his personal stamp of approval. And it’s not what you might expect if you’re thinking of the aquatic, mineral freshness of something like Acqua di Giò. I Will is a different animal—all wood and vanilla with bergamot to keep things from getting too dark and dense. It’s rich and modern and exactly what people will be talking about 20 years from now when they’re discussing the Italian fashion house’s other iconic scents.

Soft and sensual, Musc Pallida lingers like a whisper on your skin. Powdery iris and warm musk are the main components here, and they work together seamlessly. It’s light and airy but doesn’t fade quickly, and it smells good on pretty much anyone. Don’t go into Musc Pallida expecting your typical manly musk fragrance—it’s much more elegant and intimate.

Earthy sweetness, earthly delights. This fragrance from Byredo is sweet without ever venturing into gourmand territory. Even with its Madagascar vanilla and vanilla liquor accord at the base, it is punctuated with fougères and florals that give it complexity without steering it into being a spice bomb or a cognac cocktail. Instead it has a bright greenness and soft musk that can see the light of day and what’s to come. Simply fresh, refined with some sweetness—for a beautiful day.

A sophisticated blend of cinnamon, sage, and bergamot gives this fragrance a warm, spicy character. Cinnamon fragrances tend to be overpowering and reserved for nighttime, but this scent feels sophisticated and is a great daytime spice since the spicy top notes are balanced by a vanilla and praline base. It’s an elegant take on a spice-forward scent and a Nordstrom exclusive, which makes sense since Parfums de Marly is one of the retailer’s top-selling brands.

Creed’s Iris Debonair—a totally new offering, not a new expression—walks a wonderful line. It’s very gentlemanly with its cool and classic fougère heart. The opening and its transition into the middle is a lot of bergamot, absinthe, and lavender, making it seem like an expected (albeit exceptional) barbershop scent. But as it settles, iris starts to come to the forefront. It’s not the old-fashioned iris of Green Irish Tweed but a softer one that pairs well with a soft ambrette dry down. It has a true masculine and a true feminine side, and neither controls your lasting memory of the scent. Totally captivating.

Citrus and amber are not natural bedfellows, but this scent—a more highly concentrated version of the former Le Gemme Tygar—makes a very strong case for their marriage. It’s bright and grapefruit-y in one instant and then rich and woody in another. The more you smell it, the more the moniker starts to make sense. It is, after all, named after tigereye, a gemstone revered not for its sameness but for the striking impression that its contrasting bands of color create.

Fig fragrances are having a moment, but this new Granado EDP blows all other fig scents out of the water. Candied green fig reveals rich, jammy notes that are sweet but not in the typical syrupy way; it’s a lush, even fresh kind of sweet. Once you get hints of sweet dates, florals, and woods, it all combines to create a beautifully creamy gourmand.

It opens fruity and floral. A burst of raspberry gives way to peony, geranium, jasmine, magnolia. That might sound a little tough for some guys—but remember it’s just the beginning. Those notes soon give way to a base of deep, powdery musks. It’s not your typical masculine fragrance; it’s something a little more delicate and, though the scent is rooted in centuries of Chinese tradition, modern. We’d encourage you to give it a try. You might just find a new daily driver.

Before you spritz this woody scent, you have to admire the artistry of the bottle. Mind Games is known for its chess-inspired bottles, and Knight’s Tour is a great example of that. The scent inside is a sophisticated blend of lavender and saffron with spicy accents up top and smooth woods at the base. It’s a great go-to option for every day.

One Esquire editor made Ripe her scent of the summer after it launched in the spring. The reason? Its perfect balance of warm florals, fresh fruity notes, soft woods, and musks is lush and enticing. Have you ever thought about how notes of lychee, watermelon sorbet, green leaves, rose water, and creamy sandalwood work together? Spoiler alert: It’s pretty awesome.

Ojai is like waking up in a luxury hotel in California, sunlight peeking through the shades as fruity and floral notes dance around the room. Juicy, ripe mango and honey-like saffron are at the heart of this fragrance, giving it a sweet yet elevated quality. But it also has an earthy, amber-y depth to it, thanks to patchouli and frankincense at the base.

Put rhubarb in a fragrance and we’re there. Demon Dancer takes the tartness of rhubarb and blackcurrant and adds bright citrus, fresh peppermint, and radiant orange blossom for an exciting opening before it settles into a woody base of amber, musk, and tonka bean. When the cool, fresh top and heart notes reveal the warm base, it really is something to take in.

Imagine that feeling when you’re walking around a smog-choked city and find an unexpected oasis. It’s fresh, green, but with just that tiniest hint of something darker and deeper. That’s this scent. It’s wood and pepper and greenery and a reminder that even in the midst of urban sprawl, we can always find a way back to nature.

The Dolce & Gabbana classic is now available as an alcohol-free perfume gel, making it a travel-friendly option for sensitive skin. The curved metal applicator makes it easy to apply the fragrance directly to pulse points, while the pocket-sized packaging is perfect for on-the-go touch-ups. The gel formula also works as a great base for layering if you already own the original fragrance and want to extend the wear time of the cult-favorite citrus scent.

#6813. Meet Melat Kiros, the 29-Year-Old Bringing Gen Z Politics to Congress

After losing her law-firm job over her stance on Gaza protests, she turned generational frustration into a historic win.

In 2023, Melat Kiros was fired. She was 26 and worked at a major New York law firm, but she refused to condemn an open letter addressed to dozens of law firms that had urged law school deans to crack down on antisemitism on university campuses, at a time when students were protesting the slaughter in Gaza. Kiros condemned antisemitism, but warned law firms, including the one she worked for, against confusing “bigotry with the geopolitical question of Israel’s legitimacy.” This cost her her job.

She, however, was not discouraged, as she belongs to a generation that is no longer willing to set aside its values for the sake of a career. “The right to question not only the legitimacy of a government but also its actions is the bedrock principle of a true democracy,” she said of her decision. Since then, Kiros has devoted herself more intensely to politics, and yesterday, in the midterm elections, she won as Colorado’s representative to the House of Representatives. Before her, only one other Gen Z representative, Democrat Maxwell Frost of Florida, had made it to Parliament in 2022, but she became the first Gen Z woman in Congress.

Melat Kiros was born in Addis Ababa, Ethiopia, but moved to Denver with her family when she was still a baby and grew up there. She graduated from Washington College in Maryland in 2018, where she studied political science and economics, before earning a law degree from the University of Notre Dame in 2022. Today she is a lawyer and is pursuing a doctorate in public policy. In the meantime, however, she also works as a barista. It is not easy to balance a political career with her studies, but it is worth it.

After being fired, Kiros returned to Colorado and, in 2024, joined the election campaign of Democratic congressional candidate John Padora as communications director. It was excellent training for her, so much so that, two years later, she decided to run for Congress herself. On her side, she has strong political positions—she supports universal health care and child care, but also more politically controversial issues, such as abolishing Immigration and Customs Enforcement (ICE) and ending U.S. military aid to Israel. Her ideals openly align with her actions, and in fact during the election campaign she refused funding from corporate PACs and pro-Israel groups, while also sharply criticizing her Democratic primary opponent Diana DeGette, who had instead accepted donations from defense contractors as well as energy and pharmaceutical companies.

It is clear that Kiros's mark is different: She shows us what Gen Z can and will be able to do when it is given space. In this sense, her election also fits well into the climate of student protests, in Europe and around the world, by a generation that wants to make its voice heard. Yet it is still not easy: According to research by Future Caucus, more than 100 Generation Z candidates ran in the 2026 midterm elections for Congress, but the study found that young candidates were less likely to make it to the general election than other age groups, with only 24 of them appearing on the November ballots.

Kiros also had to face paternalism and dismissive attitudes from those who considered her unsuitable and inexperienced. “Now is not the time to gamble and send somebody with no experience to Washington,” DeGette had said during the primaries. Kiros, however, turned it into a strength, centering her congressional candidacy precisely on the theme of generational change. She emphasized socialist issues dear to younger people, such as affordability, social policies, climate change, countering the influence of multinational corporations, and support for students, families, and workers.

“People, I think, were really eager to see someone who is younger, who has lived experience with the economy that we're living through right now, the climate crisis that we are going to have to deal with the consequences of,” she said in an interview. For her, “socialism is about making it clear that we have basic needs for every single human being,” and she has promised to fight for this.

She also knows she has a lot to learn, but that does not stop her from walking through the doors of Congress with her head held high, without being overly intimidated by traditions and alliances. “Young people understand that we are in the fight of our lives,” she said. “We need to have leaders that are going to be fighting for the kind of bold policies that actually get something done, and they’re refusing to settle or wait for our turn or ask for permission.”

#6807. Utility Jeans Are the Coolest Denim Trend to Wear Right Now

They take you from practical to polished.

Every item on this page was chosen by an ELLE editor. We may earn commission on some of the items you choose to buy.

Often when we think about it, some of the most-worn pieces in our closets involve some form of practicality. Perhaps it’s that reversible raincoat, that cozy office-to-weekend cardigan, or a pair of utility jeans, which you may also know as cargo or work jeans.

When it comes to practicality and nonchalance, few pieces deliver quite like this one. Deep pockets, sturdy hardware, and a relaxed, easy fit mean you can run errands, commute, or settle in at a café wearing a polished version of your favorite work pants. The best pairs look just as good dressed up with a sleek knit and boots as they do dressed down with a favorite tee and sneakers, and they only get better with wear. Consider them proof that the hardest-working item in your closet can also be the most stylish.

Whether you’re after a wide-leg carpenter, a slouchy cargo, or a straight leg with just a few well-placed pockets, the right pair is the one that makes getting dressed easier. Ahead, the best utility jeans to add to your fall rotation, for a seamless blend of function and style.

This utility jean knows how to work it. Adjustable straps at the hem let you dial the tapered barrel leg up or down, proving the statement detail is as functional as it is cool. Plus, it has plenty of pockets throughout, so your favorite lip balm and other little essentials are always close at hand.

Beyond the perfect neutral shade, these jeans feature utilitarian patch pockets and knee darts that create a curvy, cohesive shape. Though roomy through the thigh and rise, they still read as sleek and slim.

A relaxed boyfriend fit and a cropped leg give way to a jean that can easily be dressed up or down with the slightest changes. Its utilitarian shape, complete with roomy patch pockets and a sturdy, broken-in feel, works just as well with a crisp white button-down and loafers as it does with a graphic tee and sneakers.

A barrel cargo is the denim combination we never knew we needed. With a dark wash, baggy fit, and snap-button pockets, this pair feels relaxed yet elevated.

We love a cuffed hem, and this particular jean does one so well. Contrasting soft gold thread runs throughout, bringing a subtle, workwear-inspired polish, while darting at the knee gives a sculpted, slightly curved effect.

With silver rivet accents and deep patch pockets, this straight-leg carpenter pant is the dressed-up cousin of the classic utility jean.

There’s something so thrilling about finding a pair of chic utility pants in a light tan shade. Made of 100 percent cotton twill, this pair is non-stretch and lightweight, making it a must for busy weekday afternoons and weekends alike.

These olive green utility jeans pair oversize pockets with a soft barrel leg for a look that’s equal parts practical and polished.

#6805. This Colorful California Mountain Home Gives Classic Ski House Style a Fresh New Look

Using the surrounding landscape as palette inspiration, designer Alden Fletcher brought 70s retro flair to this family retreat.

When most people think of a classic ski chalet, they picture log-lined walls, dark corners, a raging hearth, plenty of plaid, and maybe even a taxidermied deer or two. But a certain kind of magic strikes when high-altitude architecture hits the right notes, and this Truckee, California, retreat is a masterclass in making the mountains sing.

Built almost 20 years ago, this family holiday home boasted all the 90s mountain classics: an abundance of knotty pine, traditional slate floors, and a minimalist beige palette. Undoubtedly stylish for the time, the retreat now felt out of step with the Bay Area family looking to make it into a personalized winter escape. The owners knew updates were imminent (necessary, even), but they feared losing the home’s classic California soul. Fighting modernism’s white-box aesthetic, they called in Alden Fletcher of the eponymous San Francisco-based design firm to meld the home’s retro architecture with a fresh, forest-inspired brief.

Noting the owner’s concerns and dedication to sustainability, the project’s goal was not to remodel for grandeur, but to restore the home’s charm. Fletcher’s task was to design around storied memories, the family’s obvious love for winter sports, and their passion for entertaining—all while combining the crisp aesthetic of the alpine wilderness with cozy textiles and natural details to create the ultimate mountain sanctuary. By pulling saturated hues from the surrounding landscape and sourcing perky patterns inspired by the local culture, the redesign breathes new life into the slope-side abode without sacrificing sentiment.

FAST FACTSDesigner: Alden Fletcher Interiors Stylist: Beth Protass Location: Truckee, CAThe Space: A five-bedroom, four-bathroom home across 3,000 square feet

The home's hallways set the tone for the entire design scheme. On the first floor, just beyond the main door, wall-mounted ski runners double as decor. Upstairs, Fletcher redefined the boundaries of the primary suite to carve out a nook for a vignette displaying the home’s most bespoke decor piece: a vintage cherry-red chairlift. “The home’s location is unique,” adds Fletcher. “It sits on a ski slope, and you can view chairlifts out the back windows!”

A true gathering space, the living room was designed around the original stone-faced fireplace, though Fletcher did replace the former pine mantel with white oak, connecting the floor details with the exposed oak beams running along the ceiling.

Swiveling burnt orange armchairs accompany an inky indigo sectional. Both gather around a button-tufted ottoman, which doubles as a coffee perch or game table. Shaded sconces flank the fireplace to give the room a glow, even when the hearth is unlit, while a pair of vintage ski shoes hang on the wall, crowning the design.

In the playroom, the deep green hue of the wallpaper paired with the natural texture of knotty pine borrows inspiration from the forest-like landscape just beyond the window. Vertical lines carried through both the wallpaper and the wainscoting create the illusion of height, while playful ski motifs bring the local sport’s culture into the play space.

“The clients are sustainably minded and didn't want to get rid of what was working,” says Fletcher. With conservation in mind, she opted to keep the original perimeter cabinets, refreshing them with paint, new hardware, and Virginia Black Satin Granite countertops, a sturdier alternative to show-stopping soapstone.

To optimize the space, she nixed the two existing islands, reimagining the room to fit a larger iteration that boasts a sink and easily seats five. The design team then added upper cabinets, transforming the space into a storage haven. Later, she placed a library ladder and golden hardware for easy access and a touch of whimsy. Then, she turned her attention to making sure the lighting was up to par. Fletcher firmly believes a beautiful space should be equally as functional. To ensure no detail fell short, she installed discreet recessed lights alongside under-cabinet lighting, and two large glass pendants to adorn the space. All dimmable for ambiance—of course. “There’s nothing worse than a home kitchen that is just flooded with bright light everywhere. A kitchen should still feel cozy and warm, and good lighting is the key to accomplishing that,” says Fletcher.

Fletcher compensated for this former powder room’s lack of windows with vibrant wallpaper to energize the space and make the most of interior lighting. Borrowing space from an adjacent closet, she converted this former two-piece bath into a full retreat with a shower, vanity, and storage, finishing the space with gold hardware for an additional touch of luxury.

Perhaps the most customized room in the home, the mudroom boasts bold color and smart storage solutions for a space as functional as it is chic. The custom cabinet is an ode to smart design, featuring bench seating, pegs, an overhead shelf, and under-seat shoe storage—but the real showstopper is its bold red hue.

“My favorite element of the built-in is the color. The brick color has just the right amount of earthiness,” says Fletcher.

The family loves to entertain, often hosting large groups of friends and family. To accommodate their social lifestyle, Fletcher designed a hospitality hub. Removing a bathroom door, she altered the room’s layout, allowing space for this guest suite to come to life.

With the new dimensions secured, she covered the walls in printed wallpaper inspired by the great local outdoors. The bold pattern set the tone for the rest of the design as she pulled colors, patterns, and themes to complement it.

For the primary bedroom, the theme was warmth: a warm bed, warm home, warm soul, all channeled through muted terracotta walls. But before it could truly transform, the team had to iron out a few oddball details.

“This room used to have a few odd details, including transom windows to the adjoining bathroom and an exterior door to a deck. We removed those elements, which allowed for a better furniture layout and a cozier feeling,” says Fletcher.

The room features a traditional four-poster bed in a nearly black finish, which carries over to the lighting details and furniture hardware, bringing out the warmth and depth of the surrounding rich red hues.

For the primary bath, the team capitalized on the luxury of the sloped-side views, framing a classic 400-pound slipper tub with a nearly floor-to-ceiling window featuring vintage pine trim. Truly the focal point of the design, Fletcher finished the window treatment with woven shades to tie in the natural wood details of the home and stand up to moisture. Natural grain runs through the room’s doors and vanity, topped with soft white Calacatta marble, channeling the classic ski chalet aesthetic. Gold fixtures extend from the vanity to the lighting, towel rack, and tub, for a touch of glamour.

Boasting the project’s biggest splurge, a custom bunk-trundle bed, the kid’s room combines playful hues with forest-inspired details and maximum sleepover potential. “We wanted to maximize sleeping spots in this home,” Fletcher explains. “The Casa Kids bunk bed in the kid's room was a splurge, but it can sleep 5!”

Alden Fletcher first fell in love with design while working as an art teacher and refurbishing furniture in her downtime. In 2020, she grew that passion into a full-service design firm focused on bringing functionality to storied designs and creating ultra-personalized interiors.

And as a tasty bit of lagniappe, Representative Richard Hudson, the chairman of the National Republican Congressional Committee, lost his seat in North Carolina. (North Carolina was a notable GOP bloodbath.)

#6802. Who Is Nicholas Galitzine’s Girlfriend, Cameron Valentina Eyre? Here’s What We Know

As an actor, Nicholas Galitzine wears many hats. He’s been the Prince Charming to Camila Cabello’s Cinderella, a himbo jock in Rachel Sennott’s Bottoms, and recently played Prince Adam/He-Man in Masters of the Universe. Another role you may not know about? Nicholas is someone’s boyfriend IRL, as he was photographed walking hand-in-hand with a mystery woman in New York City on November 3.

The woman in question is dancer and model Cameron Valentina Eyre, who he’s been quietly dating for a minute. While neither of them has publicly disclosed the nature of their relationship, I must admit they look really cute together (and this definitely isn’t the first time they’ve been seen together, either—please see their London outing in March 2026). Here are all the facts on Cameron so you can alert your group chats that Nicholas is off the market.

…why wouldn’t she be? Cameron is signed to The Industry Model Management in London and NEXT Management in Paris. According to her portfolio, she’s been featured in Vogue Italia and has modeled in campaigns for Tom Ford, ASOS, and Anastasia Beverly Hills.

Honestly, the lore runs deep. Cameron was part of a girl group called CuteBad, which was only active from 2022 to 2023. They went on an indefinite hiatus when they dropped their last single, “Provocateur,” in April 2023.

According to her IMDb profile, Cameron was a dancer in the movie. The more you know!

Cameron was born on April 16, 2000, making her an Aries. Oh, and fun fact: She shares a birthday with Pope Benedict XVI and Charlie Chaplin, according to this Instagram post. Cool-cool-cool.

While the actor has kept his love life pretty private, he shocked fans when he randomly dropped a photo of him holding someone’s hand on his Instagram Stories in March 2024.

However! According to an interview with The Cut published that same month, Nicholas confirmed he had been dating someone for about six months.

“I thought the love affairs of my youth had quashed the hopeless romantic in me, but I think that will always remain,” he said at the time, adding that his girlfriend is a “flower person,” and that he realized flowers were “fucking great” that Valentine’s Day.

During a June 2026 appearance on the Therapuss podcast with Jake Shane, Nicholas made rare comments about his relationship with Cameron after he had accidentally posted a pic of her on his main Instagram Story in May.

“That was kind of supposed to be to my Close Friends,” he explained. “Look, anyone who follows me at all knows that I’ve had a girlfriend for a few years, but we like to be private, which I think is just easier in this day and age.”

Private or not, they’re still a really cute couple!

#6801. Stanley Tucci Reveals His One Holiday Tablescape Rule That’s Not What You’d Expect

His holiday palette surprisingly restrained.

Hosting on an ordinary night can be stressful, but holiday hosting takes it to another level. Between curating the perfect themes, taking time to honor every tradition, and packing the schedule full of activities, it’s easy to muddle your aesthetic amid all that prescribed cheer.

Luckily, there are easy fixes. Actor, filmmaker, and trendsetter Stanley Tucci recently sat down with House Beautiful to share his top tips for hosting with style. His holiday hot take? One traditional holiday hue should play a supporting role, not take center stage.

“I love autumnal colors, so I do that, obviously, a lot. We'll do that for Thanksgiving, then Christmas will be more white and green, with some red, but not too much,” says Tucci.

An arbitrator of taste on and off-screen, Tucci believes the only place red hues should take on the holiday table is in their natural habitat, perched on a branch.

“Berries on branches. That's all the red you need,” he says.

Tucci describes his hosting style as classic, simple, and timeless. From the menu to the tablecloth, he steers clear of flashy, overdone clichés—which is reason enough for you to ignore the trend cycle this holiday season (looking at you, Ralph Lauren Christmas), and decorate with what you already own and love.

This year marks the fifth anniversary of Tucci’s partnership with S.Pellegrino, which began with an annual limited-edition bottle featuring his engraved signature. The line has since expanded to include “Dinner Dialogues,” with conversation starters on each label. The 2026 collection continues the concept, with designs inspired by Tucci’s minimalist, curated style. It features three bespoke labels—and the actor admits he has a favorite.

“‘What memory makes you smile?’ I love this one,” Tucci says.

With a little help from Tucci, your table’s style—and the conversation around it—will be elevated and effortless.

#6799. Rails Is the Cool Fashion Brand Celebrities Wear—and Editors Gift

Give something cozy and luxe from the label Taylor Swift and J.Lo love.

Rails built its reputation on casual plaid shirts, but its sweaters, jackets, dresses, and casual tops have kept fashion insiders coming back for more than a decade. These aren’t cashmere and silk items recipients will save for special occasions; they’re pieces they’ll rely on for everyday wear.
