#6779. How to Get Chocolate Chrome Nails, Hailey Bieber’s Go-To Fall Manicure

Brown polish and chrome powder will never go out of style.

We may earn commission from links on this page, but we only recommend products we love. Promise.

In the unending trend cycle, there’s one fall manicure that has been trending every year for almost half a decade: chocolate chrome. “I think the secret behind the popularity of chocolate brown shades with chrome powder is how they give a familiar color palette a completely new look,” Los Angeles-based nail artist Vikki Swan says. “Chrome creates a beautiful wet-look effect with delicate shine and soft reflections.”

Our Lady of Unending Nail Inspiration, Hailey Bieber, has been a fan of brown chrome nails for years: She went with more of an iced latte shade in September 2022 (the same month she wore the cooler taupe version in the photo below) and I found evidence of her wearing a deeper chocolate glaze in 2022, 2023, 2024, 2025, and, most recently, about a month ago. Bieber is known for her clean-girl aesthetic, which brown chrome definitely can fit into, but when done with a dark base polish the look can feel almost edgy—more glam rock than soft girl.

That’s all to say, chocolate chrome nails are versatile, with about a million variations that look great on every nail length and skin tone. Here, some expert tips for getting the look yourself, plus a handful of inspiration photos to bring to the salon.

“Chocolate chrome” follows the time-honored tradition of naming beauty looks after food, but these manicures are simply brown nail polish (usually gel) with chrome powder on top. Still, that basic combination can lead to some very decadent final results. “Chocolate chrome takes the richness of espresso and transforms it into the shine of molten jewelry,” says Tucson-based nail artist Max Estrada. “It's glamorous, seductive and beautifully indulgent.”

Estrada sees this year’s chocolate chromes as emblematic of a larger beauty trend. “I'm seeing a move away from apologetically understated manicures toward luxury that feels almost theatrical,” Estrada says. “Think New York or Paris in 1987 with extravagant faux-fur coats, vintage Schiaparelli, opulent dresses and a cloud of Dior Poison.”

With so many shades of brown and so many chrome powders to choose from, picking one for your own chocolate manicure can feel overwhelming. The first step is deciding what vibe you want. “Darker browns create a richer, more dramatic effect, while lighter shades give a softer, more delicate finish,” Swan says, noting that she generally prefers browns with a neutral or slightly warm undertone for this.

Whether you go with a nearly-black cocoa or a creamy caramel as your base, Estrada recommends chromes that lean more gold than silver: Think champagne, bronze, or copper (though “an airy pearl chrome adds a cooler editorial twist”). Regardless of which color you prefer, Swan says to look for a sheer chrome with a delicate pearlescent finish. “They enhance the depth of the base color without covering or overpowering the original gel polish shade,” she explains.

Brown chrome also works well in a mismatched manicure, another example of nails trending toward the theatrical. “Let's be extravagant!” Estrada says. “Mix chocolate chrome with black-cherry velvet cat-eye, deep oxblood French tips, tortoiseshell, baroque gold detailing, sculpted accents and tiny crystals.” Swan also loves brown chrome paired with tortoiseshell and gold. “The key is to stay within a cohesive color palette so the different designs complement each other without making the manicure look too busy,” she says.

While it is possible to do chrome over regular polish, the look is more commonly created with a gel set, since the base color needs to be totally dry before you rub the chrome on top. “I recommend applying chrome powder after curing, while the top coat is still warm,” Swan says. “After application, it’s important to seal the chrome thoroughly with a finishing top coat to minimize the risk of chipping and ensure long-lasting results.”

Both Swan also emphasizes the importance of having a smooth base under the chrome, which requires thorough nail preparation. “Chrome powder can highlight even the smallest imperfections,” she says. “Proper nail architecture and proportions are essential, along with a high-quality top coat that creates a perfectly smooth surface.”

While chocolate chrome can work on any nail length, Estrada thinks they’re especially luxurious on longer tips. “I love these colors on a long structured coffin, exaggerated almond or beautifully sharp stiletto,” he says, noting that long nails make the decadent finish even more striking. “This winter, I want excess, glamour and artistry. Too much is precisely the point!”

Kara McGrath is a contributing beauty editor at Cosmopolitan with over 15 years of experience covering the beauty space and an at-home nail art enthusiast. Both jobs include browsing and saving countless manicure ideas for research, plus chatting with professional artists for insight into current nail trends.

In addition to Cosmo, her writing has appeared in Allure, The New York Times, Marie Claire, Business of Fashion, Playboy, NH Magazine, and more. Kara lives in New Hampshire with her dog, four cats, and a rotating cast of foster animals. #6777. The Best Early Black Friday Clothing Deals 2026 Are Here

Before you start questioning your own reality, Black Friday is still a ways away. But it's not too early to start taking advantage of some early Black Friday clothing deals that already sprouting up. Retailers seem more eager than ever to get the sales started early this year, and at this rate, Black Friday will be here before you know it. And some of the hardest-hitting discounts land before the official day even kicks off.

And it’s not just like the stalwarts like Gap, Banana Republic, Abercrombie and J.Crew in the mix, although they are heavily in the mix. Killer indies like Lost & Found and Haven also have a host of discounted goods, and outdoor behemoth REI is feeling especially generous for early November. So, if you’re trying to shop away the daylight savings blues or just feel like getting a jump on holiday shopping, you’re in the right spot.

Abercrombie doesn’t have any specific promotions that are live at the moment, but the sale section was recently stuffed with some ideal fall building blocks—most notably some baggy cords—that will make a refresh as easy and affordable as you’d ever hoped it could be.

Banana Republic has been on a heater recently, and the perfectly-curated Archive Reissue collection is leading the charge this season. It should also be noted that anything on the site that is on sale also gets an additional 20% off.

Don’t tell anyone at Gap that Black Friday is a ways off, because there are a ridiculous number of in-season pieces up to 50%—sweaters, flannels, cords, jeans, jackets, and more—and it feels like raising too much awareness to that might cause some reconsideration and none of us want that.

Haven isn’t promoting a sale anywhere on the site and you might have to dig a little bit depending what size you wear, but if you’re looking for some of the sickest IYKYK brands on the planet at insane discounts, there aren’t many places I’d rather start.

If you’re not familiar with Huckberry, they preside over one of most thoughtfully curated assortments of menswear, footwear and gear anywhere, whether that’s IRL or URL. They haven’t kicked off anything Black Friday specific yet—again, it’s early—but if you haven’t gone through the sale tab in a while, you’re missing out on some preposterously good deals.

Nearly all of J.Crew’s spring-summer collection is on sale at the moment, over 300 pieces up to 40% off through May 26—including a whole bunch of heat-beating shirting, lightweight pants, archive classics and basically anything else you’d want to be on sale.

The first name in denim has a pretty nifty “buy two, get 30% off” promotion going on site-wide, so if you’re searching for some jeans, you might just want to buy a couple pairs. (Or maybe a trucker. Or sherpa-lined trucker. You’re in charge.)

Diving into Lost & Found’s sale section is one of the more enjoyable shopping experiences one can have online right now: the selection is great, the brand list is a top-five worldwide and there’s plenty of inventory, which puts it in stark contrast with some of the less rewarding sale sections.

One of the most quietly great, easy-to-wear brands in the game recently reloaded the sale section, and basically no category was left unturned. Of particular note, are the killer sweatshirts currently available with a healthy discount applied.

Mr Porter’s sale section is the kind you can lose an hour in without realizing it, even months after a seasonal sale has ended. Case in point: there’s roughly 500 pairs of shoes and 900 jackets on sale at this very moment, and while sizes might be hit and miss, the deals aren’t.

REI’s Deals section always lives up to the billing, but right now it’s about as good as it’s ever been. Hundreds of pieces from heavyweights like Arc’teryx, Patagonia, The North Face and more are discounted up to 50%, including plenty of winter-specific gear for anyone lacking in that department.

Todd Snyder presumably won’t be partaking in the Black Friday festivities this year—they announced a new sale structure a while back—but that doesn’t mean the already-on-sale fare doesn’t hold up to the promotions sure to fire up across the board.

This year in the US, Black Friday is on Friday, November 27, a day after Thanksgiving. While that’s the official date, many retailers roll out deals throughout November. You can expect the biggest discounts to happen between Thanksgiving and Cyber Monday, but be on the look out for retailers to launch sales early.

Cyber Monday lands on November 30 this year, the Monday after Thanksgiving. Most brands keep the deals rolling through the week—but that doesn't mean a deal will last all day, so act fast.

Are clothing deals better on Black Friday or Cyber Monday?

Black Friday and Cyber Monday both deliver a plethora of clothing deals, but they play a little differently. Big-name retailers and legacy brands usually kick things off on Black Friday, while smaller menswear boutiques tend to hold their fire until Cyber Monday. Expect solid discounts starting around 20% off—and keep your eyes peeled for the coveted sale-on-sale action that’s become the norm in recent years.

In the past few months, they’ve especially connected over their “same dry and dark sense of humor,” the source said. “[They] make each other laugh.”

On November 3, Kelly and Johnson were photographed leaving a restaurant in Calabasas, California, after a dinner date. They were smiling and talking to each other in the shots.

Winter glamour, without the luxe price tag.

We may earn commission from links on this page, but we only recommend products we love. Promise.

Soft comes up in almost every review, closely followed by warm and stylish. Reviewers also said it is a bit cropped, so take that into account when picking sizing and how you want it to fit. A few also purchased the coat in multiple colors, which shows just how quality it is.

This is the perfect coat to carry with you throughout the fall and winter season when you want to elevate a simple outfit.

#6771. Hublot Just Relaunched Its First Watch. Founder Carlo Crocco Explains How It Changed Everything.

In Milan, celebrating the release of the Classic Fusion Tributo, I got to catch up with the guy who altered the aesthetics of modern watchmaking.

If you spend a lot of time following the world of watches, Hublot’s origin story might feel as familiar as one of the fairytales you heard at bedtime as a child. If you don’t, though, it might feel just as fantastical as some yarn about a princess who feels a pea under a stack of mattresses or a prince who slays a fire-breathing dragon.

You start with an unlikely hero. This time around, it’s an Italian entrepreneur with a big, bold, brazen idea. He wants to make a watch, something unlike anyone has ever seen. It’ll be gold, with an angular case shaped like the porthole on a ship. Not so crazy yet. But wait. Because, breaking entirely with the conventions of the day, the strap won’t be made of exotic leather or precious metal but rubber. Humble, industrial rubber.

This is exactly what Hublot founder Carlo Crocco envisioned nearly half a century ago, and what he brought to the Basel Watch Fair—pretty much the place to be if you wanted to be a part of the business of timekeeping—in 1980. He spent three years developing the idea, all without doing a bit of market research. He just believed in his vision. And then the fair began.

“I was very anxious, because for the first two days, no one came into our booth,” he says. But the anxiety didn’t have long to fester. Word was spreading. And then? People galore. “On the third day, we realized that everyone was speaking about us because it was really something different from all the other watches.”

That first watch—36mm in diameter, with a quartz movement and a black rubber strap—quickly became a hit. Industry buzz gave way to the kind of clientele most brands can only imagine. The King of Greece bought one in Gstaad for himself and another for his brother-in-law, the King of Spain. More royals followed. (“By the way, they’re all related,” Crocco quips.)

But the biggest thing wasn’t getting insiders to a booth at a trade fair or convincing kings to strap Hublot watches—named, if you’re curious, for the French word for porthole—on their wrists. It was changing how the industry thought about the way watches are made.

“At that time, the mentality of watchmaking was very classical,” Crocco says. “They couldn’t think, normal people, of putting together rubber and gold. I personally thought it was a really good idea because it was against the current. It was very risky, but if you don’t risk, you have no result.”

The result was a watch industry that completely recalibrated itself. Crocco says that within four or five years of the original Hublot’s release, the brand was already dealing with imitators. Now, the idea of combining disparate materials is firmly embedded in the minds of both consumers and designers. You can see it on watches from brands that sell at the local department store and brands that only sell to folks who haven’t seen the inside of one of those since the first time their private jet took off. Crocco’s big idea changed the whole game.

Which means it’s a pretty good time to go back to the beginning of this thing. Last month in Milan, Hublot debuted a new take on that first, groundbreaking watch. Dubbed the Hublot Classic Fusion Tributo, it’s an homage but not a nuts-and-bolts recreation. Instead of the original 36mm case, it’s been upsized to a more 2026-appropriate 38mm. And instead of a quartz movement—Crocco thought it was “more practical” at the time but has come around to the enduring appeal of mechanical construction—a self-winding HUB1711 movement with a 50-hour power reserve keeps things ticking along. Oh, and now you can get it in white gold, too.

Then there’s that rubber strap. When Crocco designed the original Hublot, he was insistent that the strap was entirely straight; he felt it was crucial to the aesthetic proposition. On the Tributo, it’s ever-so-slightly, almost imperceptibly tapered, a necessary concession to keep the proportions intact after increasing the case size (which, handily, lets that automatic movement sit comfortably inside).

But, as with the first Hublot, it’s not all about tech specs and watch-nerd particulars. It’s about the spirit of the thing. And the spirit of this watch stays true to that pioneering design. There are twelve screws around the bezel, so you can keep an eye on the hour despite the dial’s lack of indices—something Crocco says was a non-negotiable when he started consulting on the new release. The yellow gold version keeps the abyss-like black lacquer dial and the white gold throws us a tiny (and welcome) curveball by using onyx. The combo of precious metal and rubber still hits, 46 years later.

If you prod him how he feels about the way that he shifted the watch industry, Crocco won’t brag. He left Hublot nearly 20 years ago, when it sold to LVMH. He knows his idea changed things; he’s happy about it. But this isn’t his day job anymore. Still, when he’s asked if he could be considered the godfather of fusing high and low in watchmaking—and you give him a minute to ponder it—he’ll tell you the truth.

#6768. Asking for a Friend, Is the Silent Treatment Really That Bad?

Therapists explain why going quiet during conflict can be so damaging.

In a perfect world, we’d all be emotionally mature, communicatively adept lovergirls (gender-neutral, BTW) dating the same. But to the chagrin of the verbal processors and chatterboxes amongst us, some of us wind up with someone who goes silent in the face of strife. If you’ve ever been on the receiving end of the silent treatment and spiraled, this article is for you.

First, quick definition: “The silent treatment is unexplained, open-ended, and intentional withdrawal of connection and communication to control or manipulate the other partner,” Stacy Hubbard, LMFT, an EMDR-certified trauma therapist and certified Gottman therapist and Gottman master trainer based in Ashland, Oregon, tells Cosmopolitan.

Sure, sometimes this looks like blatant ignoring of your questions and bids for connection. But it can also mean withholding positive nonverbal responses (think: smiles, eye contact, affectionate touch) or swapping them with gestures that ooze contempt, like scoffing or eye-rolling, says Kimberly Panganiban, LMFT, a certified Gottman couples therapist based in San Diego, California. And because we live in the age of the smartphone, “it can also mean not responding to text messages,” she adds.

But now for what you’re really wondering: Why the heck does my partner go hush, and is it as bad as it feels? Ahead, relationship therapists explain what's behind the radio silence, what it means for your relationship, and how to get the chit-chat going again.

Bluntly, the silent treatment is neither a healthy nor an effective way to deal with conflict and other hard-to-swallow feelings like disappointment, anger, or hurt. “The silent treatment is concerning anytime—but it becomes especially concerning if it is used regularly,” says Panganiban.

“Being on the receiving end of the silent treatment can be very distressing for the partner on the receiving end of it,” says Panganiban. If you’ve ever begged for so much as a syllable—let alone a full-ass sentence—from someone dead-set on giving you the cold shoulder, you know how true that is.

Suddenly, the person who is supposed to be your confidant and cheerleader, first call and best friend, is as likely to answer your texts or calls as your celebrity crush is to slide into your DMs Adam Levine style with a “Holy fck. Holy fucking fck. That body of yours is absurd.” (IYKYK). With that comes an avalanche of doubt and despair, burying you in questions about where you stand and whether your relationship will weather the storm.

Through an attachment theory lens, the person who should be your secure base suddenly becomes a source of uncertainty. Over time, this can create a sense that your beloved attention, love, and care are conditional, which “will start to impact the level of trust in the relationship,” Panganiban says.

“Trust requires that both partners feel that their partner has their back at all times (even in conflict),” she explains. When one partner withdraws instead of working through the issue, the receiving partner may begin to question if their partner has their best interests at heart.

To top it off, Panganiban says the silent treatment robs a relationship of the chance to reap the bounty of healthy conflict management, including mutual understanding, emotional intimacy, and the chance to come back together even stronger.

The silent treatment works about as well as a rubber with holes in it… so why do people do it? In part, because many of us never learned how to actually resolve conflict.

Few of us took a How to Fight Better class in high school. Instead, we got our so-called “lessons” in conflict resolution from loved ones who relied on ineffective (read: downright harmful) methods to express hurt, get their point across, or meet their needs. Indeed, many adults who give the silent treatment witnessed their parents give it when they were kids, or received it themselves, according to Hubbard.

“When a child is frequently given the silent treatment, they may mimic the behavior of the parent and become the silent partner growing up,” says licensed clinical professional counselor Kristine Cain, LCPC, founder and lead therapist of Full Circle Counseling in Frankfort, Illinois.

Conversely, some children learn that the only way to restore connection is to set their own needs aside and focus all their energy on appeasing the withdrawn parent. As adults, this tendency to prioritize someone else's emotional needs over their own may leave them particularly vulnerable to relationships where they repeatedly receive the silent treatment, she explains.

Not all silence is a calculated, conniving attempt to control your partner like a marionette. Sometimes, it's a sign that someone's nervous system has gone into overdrive.

In a society that teaches us to prioritize production over pleasure, pushing through over slowing down, we aren't exactly taught to tune into our bodies… despite living in them 24/7. As a result, people aren't always aware of the physiological symptoms that signal a need to step away from an argument, Panganiban says.

She explains: When you're fighting with someone you love, your body doesn't necessarily register the disagreement as just another Tuesday-night tiff. “Our partner is the most important person in our life, so when we are in conflict with them, it feels like a threat to our safety and our bodies respond as such,” she says.

When your nervous system interprets a disagreement with a partner as a capital-T threat, “a series of physiological changes begin to occur in your body,” says Panganiban. “Your heart rate speeds up, your body secretes adrenaline and cortisol, and blood flow to the kidneys, gut, and certain parts of the brain shuts off.” (This is known as flooding).

These changes can make it harder to use healthy communication skills, like thinking clearly, listening openly, and responding empathetically. At this point, some people will respond by fighting Jersey Shore season one style, but others will go quiet…

When someone goes quiet during a conflict because their nervous system has gone haywire, it can look a lot like the silent treatment, but it might actually be stonewalling. Popularized by relationship researchers at the Gottman Institute, stonewalling is marked by shutting down and becoming unresponsive during conflict, often because they're physiologically overwhelmed (a.k.a. flooded), says Hubbard.

“Outwardly, stonewalling and the silent treatment may look the same,” she says. But the difference lies in intent. “Stonewalling occurs when someone enters into a state of diffuse physiological arousal and withdraws as an attempt to de-escalate and self-regulate,” says Panganiban. By contrast, the silent treatment may be a deliberate attempt to punish a partner, avoid accountability, or sidestep a difficult conversation, she explains.

That doesn’t mean that stonewalling gets a free pass. “Stonewalling is harmful,” says Hubbard. In fact, it's one of the Four Horsemen, the conflict patterns Gottman researchers identified as predictors of relationship breakdown. (ICYWW: The other three are contempt, criticism, and defensiveness).

Someone giving you the silent treatment may be trying to make you uncomfortable, while someone stonewalling is trying to make themselves comfortable. Whether your partner has gone mum because they're giving you the silent treatment or because they've become so overwhelmed that they're stonewalling you, neither is good news for your relationship.

Still, you don't need to know exactly why your partner is shutting you out to know that their silence is hurting you—and that you want to take steps to help stop it.

If you want to go the distance, Cain says it's worth being brave and naming how their withdrawal affects you.

She offers this formula: “When you go silent, I feel ________ (worthless, abandoned, not good enough, etc.). My _________ (mom, dad, friend, ex, etc.) used to do this and it is very triggering for me. Can we figure out a way to communicate better so that we don't get to that point again?”

Of course, you don't need a history of being given the silent treatment—or to unpack your childhood woes—to justify wanting your partner not to go AWOL on you. So, you could also keep it simple: “When you stop talking to me without explaining why, I feel shut out. I’d like us to work together to figure out a different way to handle those moments.”

The goal isn’t to say The Perfect Thing, but instead to make it clear that their silence isn’t working for you.

While you can’t know someone’s intentions just by tallying their actions, doing so can be a helpful thought exercise that helps you figure out whether or not this is a relationship you actually want long(er) term.

If your partner tends to shrink when a scuttles gets saucy, or has described feeling overwhelmed they may be familiar with flooding. On the other hand, if their silence kicks in any time you set a boundary, express a need, or do something they don't like, it may be their attempt at control.

Hubbard also suggests taking a beat to consider how your partner treats you most of the time. Does your partner regularly dismiss you or make you feel responsible for managing their moods? If so, their silence could be part of a larger pattern of controlling behavior. Conversely, if they tend to shut down during other emotionally intense situations—not just disagreements with you—that could suggest they struggle with flooding more broadly.

So you talk to your partner, and they’re receptive as hell? Hell yeah, brother!

If you and your partner—perhaps with the help of a relationship therapist, wink, wink—determine that physiological overwhelm is part of the picture, Panganiban recommends that you both get familiar with your personal warning signs.

“Recognizing flooding can be tricky, but everyone has different physiological indicators, so it is important that both partners learn what those are,” she says. For some, the giveaway is dry mouth; for others, it's muscle tension; and for many, it's a racing heart. Once you both know the signs, you can make a plan for what to do when they show up, she says.

Technically speaking, a flooding ritual is a strategy for managing flooding before it leads to stonewalling. But even if your partner's silence isn't the result of a nervous system gone haywire, the underlying principle of communicating when they need space and when you'll come back together can keep silence from jet-setting you to Spiral City.

Hubbard suggests picking a single word—essentially, a fighting safeword—that either of you can say to communicate that you need to take a pause, but will reconnect in 20 or 30 minutes. It could also be a nonverbal cue, such as a time-out hand motion, that communicates the same, says Panganiban.

During the pause, the goal is to reduce physiological arousal by doing anything that soothes you, says Panganiban. “It could be deep breathing, going on a walk, engaging in a hobby, watching TV, or anything else that gets your mind off of the conflict and brings your body back to baseline,” she says.

Panganiban says the most important part is returning to the conversation at the agreed-upon time, which she recommends be no more than 24 hours later. As Hubbard explains, “embedded into this ritual is the expectation that both partners will return to the conversation rather than leaving one person waiting indefinitely.”

Of course, even the world's most thoughtful flooding ritual can’t do diddly squat if your partner refuses to participate. And if they keep pulling the silent treatment out of their back pocket even after you’ve told them how much it hurts? Well, perhaps that says it all…

#6767. Maxx Royal Bodrum Reimagines the Mediterranean Escape

The French and Italian Rivieras have long been popular summer destinations, but there’s another scenic stretch of the Mediterranean worth your immediate attention. Located on Turkey’s southwestern coast, Bodrum is growing in popularity as a vacation spot due to its beaches, waterfront restaurants, and yacht-filled marinas. It‘s also home to Maxx Royal Bodrum, a luxury resort that opened in May 2024, with private villas, restaurants from world-renowned chefs, and an expansive spa.

Set on a private peninsula overlooking the Aegean Sea, the property is surrounded by pine trees and has its own beach. It’s the third Turkish destination from Maxx Royal Resorts, following properties in Belek and Kemer, and features 294 rooms across a collection of suites and villas.

Designed with input from Turkish architect Mahmut Anlar, the resort incorporates stone, wood, and other natural materials that complement the coastal setting. Its Island Villas, for example, take inspiration from traditional stone houses found throughout Bodrum. Designed with local stone, wood, and large windows that bring in natural light, the villas each come equipped with private gardens, terraces, and infinity pools overlooking the sea.

Guests can also choose from larger villas better suited to families or groups or suites with direct pool access. The five-bedroom Presidential Hill Villa, in particular, boasts its own heated seawater pool, fitness room, sauna, and private butler’s room.

In terms of dining, Maxx Royal Bodrum is home to Spago by Wolfgang Puck, the chef's premier restaurant concept, as well as Caviar Kaspia, which specializes in seafood and, of course, caviar.

More recent additions include Leña, a steakhouse from Spanish chef Dani García, and Safraan, which serves up seafood and Mediterranean-inspired small plates.

There’s also Seavore, for updated takes on traditional Turkish dishes, and the on-site ice cream parlor, which stocks unique flavors like Turkish pistachio and Bodrum tangerine.

For those looking to spend some time away from the beach, the Maxx WellBeing Center spans approximately 61,900 square feet and includes 20 treatment rooms. Its offerings range from traditional Turkish baths and Biologique Recherche skin care treatments to reformer Pilates, yoga, and sound healing.

At Maxxi Land, children can participate in activities organized by age group, including arts and crafts, games, and film screenings. And beyond the resort, boat excursions or visits to nearby historical destinations, including the ancient city of Ephesus, are on offer.

Getting there is relatively straightforward, too. Maxx Royal Bodrum is about an hour from from Milas-Bodrum Airport, with boat and helicopter transfers also available for the jet-set crowd.

#6766. After I Kicked Drugs and Alcohol, I Found a New Addiction: Cheating on My Fiancée

Thirteen years of sobriety taught me that the most dangerous thing for an addict isn’t getting caught—it’s getting away with it. And I keep getting away with it.

Ethical nonmonogamy relies on a foundation of total transparency. Partners openly agree to terms and continually negotiate them together. Betrayals such as infidelity can take many shapes and break the agreement a couple has made, monogamous or not. For people in recovery, however, the adrenaline of secret keeping can feel familiar. Clinicians call this behavioral, or process, addiction—in which compulsion lies in the thrill of the chase rather than the payoff. It doesn’t excuse betrayal, but it can help explain how a person committed to honesty ends up living a lie.

For this installment of the Secret Lives of Men, we spoke to Owen,* a 36-year-old brand strategist with 13 years of sobriety who spent most of the first two years of his relationship cheating on the partner he plans to marry. His story begs the question: What does it mean to carry out “living amends,” an Alcoholics Anonymous term for permanently changing your behavior for the better, when you aren’t ready to confess?

*Names and identifying details have been changed to protect anonymity.

Once a week, my fiancée has a therapy session over a video call that she takes from our bedroom. We both work from home, so while that happens, I’m usually barricaded in my office with noise-canceling headphones on, keeping up with work. Recently, I came out to the kitchen to pour a coffee after her hour was up, and she wasn’t home.

She takes walks, so there was no reason to panic. But my mind went where it has gone a lot lately: She found out. By whatever cruel trick of fate, she knew, and she was out there walking it off, letting the anger curdle. When she came back, she would bring hell with her.

I called once. She didn’t pick up. I didn’t try again. For half an hour, I paced the apartment unable to get a single thing done. I checked my phone every few seconds. I rehearsed what I’d say when she walked through the door: which parts I’d confess, which parts I’d still try to save. I worried myself sick. When she finally walked back in, it turned out she’d just been on a long phone call with her sister. She took off her headset, kissed me hello, and asked what I had in mind for dinner. My heart kept pounding long after she’d walked into the kitchen.

Until two years ago, I could say with real pride that I had never cheated on anyone. Not once. Not even when I was drinking and doing all those drugs, back when I lied about nearly everything else. I’d had opportunities, but infidelity was the one line I never crossed.

Advanced 12-step work taught me a strange paradox: Sometimes you have to sit with the agonizing truth that you are doing something wrong. That you aren’t ready or able to stop. I’ve built 13 years of sobriety on the idea that we are only as sick as our secrets. But I should admit something up front: I’m good at telling stories, especially about myself. Even now, I catch myself deciding which parts to share. So here it is: For the better part of two years, I cheated on the woman I’m going to marry. She still doesn’t know.

In recovery, you learn that the most dangerous thing that can happen to a person with an addiction isn’t getting caught—it’s getting away with it. And every time you skate by, the lie gets a little easier to tell. The disease gets a little more room to grow. I first learned that at 23. I had to learn it again in my 30s.

I come from a long line of talented, tortured people. My father sold commercial real estate and spent years in and out of a quiet, high-functioning addiction to painkillers. He went away for treatment twice. My mother was raised by an alcoholic and carried a lot of anger. My parents divorced when I was young, both remarried badly, and neither of their houses was a safe place emotionally.

At 19, while in college, I fell into a relationship I can only describe as wickedly codependent. We went through two miscarriages together and became trauma bonded. I didn’t know how to grieve, so I was drinking heavily and taking almost anything I could find: stimulants, psychedelics, dissociatives. Just before I turned 21, I caught my girlfriend in a lie so profound it ripped the wool off my eyes: She had been sleeping with my best friend for nearly the entire time we’d been together. One of the miscarried babies was actually his. I ended things the day I found out and took the situation as the perfect excuse to keep killing myself slowly.

I didn’t make it a year out of college before the consequences caught up with me. The fall after graduation, I was driving home from a concert when I got pulled over for a dead taillight. I was relatively sober and polite and was sure I’d talked my way out of it—right up until the officer asked me to empty my pockets. Inside were a bag of powder that I’d been promised was Molly and a few pills I didn’t have a prescription for. They booked me and sent everything off to the state lab. For three months, nothing happened. Meanwhile, I painted myself further into the corner before the floor ultimately fell out. When the charge finally came, I began a year and a half of court dates and probation.

As a first offender, I pled into a court-mandated, recovery-focused diversion program with regular drug tests. I followed the program to a T, at least for a while.

One weekend, when house-sitting alone for a neighbor, I took a drink out of boredom and pure spite. No test caught it. Then I lied about it to my counselors, the court, and my family. A month later, guilt finally won out, and I admitted it to my sponsor. A few days later, he brought it back up and told me I had to come clean to everyone.

“The fact that you were willing to conceal that from everyone for this long,” he said, “is the only evidence you need of how sick you are.”

The problem wasn’t the drink. It was how easily I hid it. I surrendered to the program and handed my life over to a higher power. I haven’t had a drink since.

I went celibate by choice for three years. I did a lot of therapy and learned that I had swung from codependent to avoidant. I had finally regained control, and I didn’t want to hand myself to anyone. Then, in my late 20s, I decided to give dating another shot.

Before long, I leaned into ethical nonmonogamy and dated as “solo poly,” which means I was seeing multiple people without a primary partner. Casual dating became my primary hobby. Some connections were purely physical; some were friends with benefits; some went emotionally deep. At my peak, I was sleeping with six people, two or three times a week. I felt I was winning at something. I was always honest with every partner about who I was, sobriety included. But I wasn’t honest with myself about why I needed so much of it.

Then, later in therapy, I came to understand that the dopamine that keeps people in an addiction loop is driven less by the thing itself than by the anticipation of getting it. Dating apps are built on that anticipation. For me, there were three hits: the match, the first nudes, the first hookup. In the program, we say the disease of addiction wears as many faces as it needs to. The chase was one of those faces.

I matched with Maya as she was getting ready to leave on a six-week work trip to Thailand. We messaged far longer than usual, trading long voice notes across the 11-hour time difference—her from hotel lobbies at night, me on morning walks. By the time we finally met, I already knew how she laughed. Our first date was electric, the best of my life. We sat at the bar until they started stacking chairs, talking about family, grief, books that had saved us. She was self-sufficient and emotionally deep. She was great at conversation. She was beautiful.

A few months into our relationship, we curled up on her couch on a Sunday afternoon and talked about ethical nonmonogamy. We’d both dabbled in it, but neither of us had done it inside a primary relationship. I told her I wanted it. She was open to it. We agreed to start monogamous and build the foundation first, but we never said for how long.

The truth is, our sex wasn’t great. Early on, I suggested something new in bed, and she went quiet and turned toward the wall. Neither of us brought it up the next morning. I internalized the idea that I couldn’t ask for what I wanted, that this was just how it would be until we opened things up. Instead of communicating, I let the resentment build.

Then, one afternoon at a coffee shop across town, I ran into Bree, a woman I used to hook up with. She had been the perfect friend with benefits: incredible chemistry but no desire for a relationship. We stayed and talked for an hour. When she mentioned she lived ten minutes away, neither of us pretended not to know what that meant. By that evening, I was at her place. It was the first time in my life I had been unfaithful in any way, and I felt awful. When I went home, I waited for the consequences. They never came.

A big part of addiction is justification. We were planning to be nonmonogamous, I told myself, so the way I’d just conducted myself was basically equivalent. It wasn’t that bad. I can see now how thin that logic was. Nonmonogamy runs on transparency and consent. I was keeping a secret.

Over the next year and a half, my infidelities came and went. A few more times with Bree. A handful of times with anonymous women in other cities. Trans women, sex workers. I traveled for work, and I took advantage of the distance. I kept my phone face down, hid dating apps, used end-to-end-encrypted texting. For each time I followed through, there were five times I stopped myself. Each close call made me feel like I had it under control.

Strangely, the woman I cheated with most also kept me from making worse mistakes. Bree became my confidante, the only person I was fully honest with about any of it. One night, I arranged to meet a sex worker. I was sitting in my car outside the hotel when clarity hit. The only thing I could think to do was call Bree. She talked me down. She and I ended up having sex that night. The program has a phrase for that kind of thinking: I want what I want when I want it.

Meanwhile, the relationship I was betraying kept getting better. We slowly began to practice nonmonogamy, and it took us far longer than either of us expected. We listened to the audiobook version of Polysecure by Jessica Fern together. We’d pause so often to talk that a half-hour chapter took two hours. It gave me a way to ask for what I wanted without it feeling like an attack on her. One night, Maya paused the book and asked me, plainly, how she could do better. For the first time, I believed she meant it, and I told her. The sex got better. Then, much better.

The final close call came in June. Bored at my desk, I connected with someone on an anonymous hookup site, and we moved to texting. A few messages in, after I’d sent a couple of nudes, my phone lit up with a long blackmail threat. Attached was a screenshot of Maya’s Instagram. This kind of thing had happened four or five times before, back when I was single. Go ahead, I would say. I never considered paying, and no one ever followed through. But this time, I closed my laptop, walked to the bathroom, sat on the edge of the tub until my hands stopped shaking, and blocked the number. This time, I had something to lose. I was forced to face how stupid I was being.

I shared the story in therapy. My therapist, who usually lets me do most of the talking, leaned back, looked at his clipboard, and said, “What the fuck are you doing? This is like the 20th time something like this has happened.”

I didn’t have an answer. Nothing had ever happened to me. That was the whole point.

This past August, on a weekend trip to the Catskills with friends, I proposed to Maya. She said yes before I could finish the question. I meant every word I said that weekend.

Maya and I have now officially opened our relationship. We’ve mostly played as a couple. When she sees someone on her own, she tells me everything the next morning. Last month, I sexted with a woman for days and chose not to tell her. Though I have permission, I can’t seem to give up the secrecy.

Growing up in the houses I did, I hid the truth to keep the peace. Recovery taught me to say it anyway, but I’ve only ever practiced with people who can’t leave, like a sponsor or a therapist. Telling Maya is different. Sometimes, I reverse the question: What if she were the one carrying a secret like this? I like to think that I’d find a way to accept it, because through everything I’ve done, I have never loved her any less.

In the program, when you can’t make amends without causing further harm, you make what’s called living amends. It starts with stopping the behavior. Then you try to love better, act cleaner, and stay present day after day, without asking for absolution. That’s where I am now. I pray regularly that this pain is my consequence. If that’s my penance, I’ll carry it.

#6765. Erika Felt the “Math Wasn’t Really Mathing” With Blake’s Finances During ‘Love Is Blind’

There was a lot more to their breakup than we saw.

Erika and Blake were one of the many (many!) couples to end Love Is Blind Season 11 without getting married, and apparently there was more to their split than we saw on screen.

The show’s finale gave viewers a brief glimpse at Erika and Blake ending their relationship seemingly due to logistical issues: he wanted to live on a boat and sail around the world, while she wanted to live in a house and start a family. But Erika says there were some other red flags.

“I was like, ‘There’s no way this guy’s broke.’ He’s got a career and not a lot of bills from what I heard. So the math wasn’t really mathing,” she told Tudum. “Those are questions that maybe I was too afraid to ask at the beginning. They’re very sensitive and personal. But I remember him being so cool about [finances] that it almost felt like he did have money in the moment. He wasn’t sweating about it at all.”

She added that “Finances are something that, over time, you start to see some patterns and traits. Like, ‘What credit cards are they using? Are they paying with cash?’”

On top of that, Erika felt like she hadn’t really gotten to experience Blake’s world and didn’t know what she was jumping into. She literally thought to herself, “‘I can’t marry you, I haven’t seen enough of your life,’” adding, “It just started to feel really dangerous to get any closer to the wedding having already been through a divorce.”

Love Is Blind was filmed over a year ago, and if you’re wondering where things stand with these two now...we have spoilers below!

#6763. No-Knead Pumpkin Focaccia

For those who fall into the pumpkin-should-be-savory camp, let us offer up a new recipe primed to become a seasonal staple: Pumpkin Focaccia. The iconic bread we know and love is ripe for the pumpkin treatment, lending it a golden hue and earthy sweetness. This version doesn’t require any kneading, making it straightforward enough to pull off in a single afternoon. You’ll perform a series of folds as it rises, simply pulling and stretching the dough to help it develop gluten. A showering of fresh rosemary and flaky salt on top is optional, but it takes crisp, plush focaccia from good to great. Serve it with a bowl of olive oil for dipping or a schmear of cream cheese to lean into the seasonal profile.

This recipe is part of Pumpkin Week 2026, Bon Appétit’s annual celebration of autumn’s most magical pantry staple.

Mix one ¼-oz. envelope active dry yeast (about 2¼ tsp.), 5 cups (625 g) all-purpose flour, 1 cup canned pumpkin purée, 1 Tbsp. Diamond Crystal or 1¾ tsp. Morton kosher salt, 1 Tbsp. light brown sugar, and 1½ cups warm water in a large bowl until a dry, shaggy dough forms. Cover with plastic wrap and let sit in a warm spot 15 minutes.

Place a small bowl of room-temperature water on work surface for easy reaching. Uncover dough and moisten hands in water, shaking off excess. Stretch 1 side of dough up and over to meet opposite side; turn bowl 90°. Repeat process 3 more times, turning bowl after each fold. (This constitutes 1 full fold.) If dough doesn’t always stretch to meet opposite side, just aim for the middle. Remoisten your hands and shake off excess water any time dough begins to feel sticky. Cover dough and let sit 30 minutes (set a timer). Repeat entire process 2 more times (folding and resting), about 1 hour more. By the last full fold, the dough should be significantly smoother and tighter.

Pour ¼ cup extra-virgin olive oil into a 13x9" metal baking pan. Lightly oil your hands and punch down dough, then transfer to prepared pan. Sprinkle with flaky sea salt. Turn dough salted side down to coat both sides in oil. Press into an even layer with your fingers. Cover with plastic wrap and let sit in a warm spot until puffy, about 45 minutes.

Place a rack in bottom third of oven; preheat to 450°. Unwrap focaccia and scatter rosemary leaves and more sea salt over if desired; drizzle with oil. Oil your hands and press fingers into focaccia several times to create dimples across surface, making sure to press fingers all the way down to the bottom of the pan. Bake focaccia, rotating pan front to back halfway through, until deep golden brown, 23–28 minutes. Transfer pan to a wire rack and let focaccia cool slightly in pan. Using a metal spatula, turn out focaccia onto rack and let cool completely.

Do Ahead: Focaccia can be made 1 day ahead. Store tightly wrapped at room temperature.

#6762. Esquire First Look: How Bad Monkey Season 2 Brews Up Even More Trouble in Paradise

Creator and showrunner Bill Lawrence reveals how John Malkovich’s love of the Apple TV crime series starring Vince Vaughn landed him a role as the “comedic and dangerous” new villain.

For Bill Lawrence, producing some of TV’s biggest comedies is serious business. Once the architect of cable juggernauts Scrubs and Cougar Town, the 57-year-old producer is now at the helm of several streaming-era darlings, including Ted Lasso, Shrinking, Rooster, and Bad Monkey. But of all his shows, Lawrence tells Esquire that Bad Monkey scratches a specific itch.

“My favorite movies, books, and TV shows that grabbed me as a kid melted genres,” Lawrence says. A self-professed diehard for buddy-cop classics like 48 Hrs. and Beverly Hills Cop, the two-time Emmy winner says that the films’ signature mixture of humor with grittier themes and settings made them more than just funny movies. “People remember them as raucous comedies, and they were. But there were real emotional stakes,” says Lawrence. “And the first author that got me into that stuff was Carl Hiaasen.”

A political journalist and author from Florida (where Lawrence’s family originates), Carl Hiaasen wrote the satirical crime novel Bad Monkey in 2013. The book was adapted in full in 2024 as the Apple TV series’ first season. Lawrence was, and is, a “massive fan” of Hiaasen’s. “Then I got to know this guy,” he says. “That one of my heroes turned out to be like I hoped he would be, and that he let me take one of his best properties and turn it into a little bit of throwback TV—it’s one of the most fun rides of my career.”

Now Lawrence is ready to unpack the upcoming second season of Bad Monkey—and its Emmy-worthy newcomers—which returns to Apple TV on December 2. A new trailer, launching today, is embedded just below.

Bad Monkey season 2 sees Vince Vaughn returns as Yancy, a former Miami detective turned reluctant restaurant inspector who lives in the Florida Keys. With severed arms and medical fraud deep in his rearview mirror, Yancy finds himself caught in a new case involving a shady restaurant—an obvious front for a criminal enterprise—and the underworld boss behind it. Three-time Emmy Award winner and Oscar nominee John Malkovich joins as a new antagonist, Spencer. While Lawrence resists revealing too much about Spencer, he describes the role as a “comedic and dangerous” villain who drives Yancy to the brink.

“One of my favorite things about Carl’s books is that they’re really, at their core, character stories,” Lawrence says. “Spencer” is a role that Lawrence and his team “reverse-engineered” for Malkovich when they caught wind of the actor praising Bad Monkey while promoting his 2025 film Opus. “Randomly, Malkovich said, ‘My favorite show this year was Bad Monkey,’ ” Lawrence recalls. “This story started from a place of [thinking], If John Malkovich was kind and silly enough to bring him in this world, who would he be? You will meet him as our other fearless leader this year.”

The second season of Bad Monkey breaks from Hiaasen’s books (with the author’s blessing) to tell a new story that, in a spiritual sense, takes place between the original Bad Monkey novel and its 2016 sequel, Razor Girl. “We took massive liberties expanding certain characters, making the world bigger,” Lawrence says about collaborating with Hiaasen. “My nightmare would be to do anything he didn’t feel was true to Florida or his voice. I’m not overstepping when I tell you he helped come up with the main issue that Rosa’s chasing down [in season 2], because it was something he had written about when he was a journalist.”

Adds Lawrence, “It was nerve-racking. One of the scariest things, if you’re doing an IP [series] and it’s your favorite author, [is when you] have to make up things, but I’m very lucky he’s become a close friend and is candid with me to tell me if we hit or miss on it.”

Malkovich isn’t the only new face in season 2. A slew of stand-up comics, up-and-coming actors, and notable names fill out the ensemble. Sam Jaeger, Zavior Phillips, Peter Billingsley, Sofia Boutella, and Mo Amer all appear in the second season, to name a few. The Handmaid’s Tale’s Yvonne Strahovski also joins as a series regular in a role Lawrence hints has “something much bigger and deeper going on.” Returning characters from season 1 include Rosa (Natalie Martinez), Ro (John Ortiz), and Caitlin (Charlotte Lawrence, Bill’s real-life daughter).

But Bad Monkey is still all about Vince Vaughn’s Yancy, the endlessly witty, if also damaged, soul of the whole shebang. Yancy has changed a little since season 1—“He realized that he was a catalyst for change in other people,” Lawrence says—but he’s also the same dude who knows how to relax in his own backyard. “He was still drifting around and ended up very alone. Were we to have him be the same loner throughout season 2, it would be a mistake. He is the same Yancy. But watching that character decide what it means to let people into his life—whether it be Caitlin or Rosa—that is his personal journey. I wouldn’t want to watch the show if it weren’t Yancy being Yancy on some level.”

Yancy’s big story for season 2 involves his father, Jim, played again by Scott Glenn, whom Yancy invites to stay with him in his home. Lawrence says this story aims to highlight Yancy’s internal struggle as a budding father figure. “We all have dynamics with our fathers—me, Vince, [co-executive producer] Matt [Tarses]. You become your parent’s ... I don’t want to say caretaker, but the roles switch. That’s a role that comes your way. The person who always could take care of you, you need to step up and do that for them and embrace taking their role for everyone else.”

“If you ask me what [season 2] is actually about,” says Lawrence, “It’s about Yancy stepping in to be the patriarch of whatever his found family is, and whether or not he is ready to do that.”

If one thing sets Bad Monkey apart from Lawrence’s other works, it’s action. Lawrence aims to pay proper homage to the movies that influenced him with “feature-level” set pieces this year. “In season 1, we were flying by the seat of our pants, seeing what we could do, especially in Florida,” says Lawrence. “The one thing we wanted to upgrade in season 2 is the action. It’s stuff I haven’t shot in my normal 30-minute comedies before, and I think we pulled it off.”

The action will be felt through the characters—the ones standing, anyway. Bill Lawrence teases that the sheer amount of newcomers means one or two or several won’t make it to the end. “One of the things about Carl’s books is that when you reach the end, a lot of the regulars have died. Knowing the cast will never get too big, it’s fun to see the smoke clear and [find out] who’s left.”

This is serious business. But it’s still a blast.

#6761. The Women Who “Stopped the Wedding”—and Never Looked Back

“Ihad already bought my wedding dress, asked my bridesmaids, and started imagining what was supposed to be one of the happiest days of my life,” 27-year-old Sacha Smith tells me. “I just didn’t know that the relationship I believed I was in wasn’t the reality.”

After discovering her fiancé had been cheating on her for the entirety of their engagement, Smith called off the wedding. She is one of hundreds of women who—as part of a viral TikTok trend—shared their story of coming to their senses and ending their relationship right before a wedding.

In these TikTok videos, women share photos and videos of themselves in their previous relationships—sometimes even trying their wedding dresses on—with the words of Ashe’s viral song “Stop the Wedding!” playing in the background. In the song, Ashe sings, “Even though the table’s set, the guests are waiting, you can burn the dress and run away. Stop the wedding.” The song is catchy, but it’s also a warning cry to women that it’s never too late to save yourself from a lifetime of misery tied to the wrong person.

And it often actually takes something as monumental as an impending wedding to figure out a relationship isn’t working. “When you’ve spent years overriding your own feelings to maintain relationships, it can become really difficult to recognize what you actually want,” Cheryl Groskopf, LMFT, tells Cosmopolitan. “A wedding makes everything feel real and tangible because now you’re facing the possibility of committing to this dynamic for the rest of your life. And sometimes that forces you to confront something you’ve been trying really hard to avoid.”

This was the case for Katherine Woller, 39. There was no cheating partner or big fallout—just a gut feeling she couldn’t ignore. The feeling started the moment her boyfriend got down on one knee. Even with the gorgeous backdrop of Champagne, France, she couldn’t shake the thought that she was making a mistake by saying yes.

It was only two weeks before her wedding when Woller stood in front of the mirror wearing her would-be wedding dress during a fitting, feeling utterly detached. “I felt completely out of control and lost, as if I were watching someone else’s life,” she says. “At that moment, I couldn’t ignore my inner voice any longer and knew it was time to make a big, hard choice.”

Despite the magnitude of an “I shouldn’t be doing this” feeling, a wedding can make it seem impossible to walk away. This is called a sunk cost fallacy. And even though you understand (via its label) that it isn’t true, in the moment, it feels like you’ve put too much in to the relationship to leave it. Therapist Justena Kavanagh, LCSW, explains that once you’re engaged, the deposits, the guest list, and other people’s expectations build momentum. “Stopping can feel harder than going through with it.”

Tishayla Williams was 30 when she was engaged. She had bought the dress, finalized the venue, and was the only woman in her family who wasn’t married or a mother. The pressure was very real.

But when she had been followed out of a cafe by a strange man and called her fiancé crying, his first response had been, “What were you wearing?”

“I told him to come collect his two-carat engagement ring,” she says.

When your gut is screaming at you that you’re with the wrong person, ignoring it feels like abandoning yourself. This is why so many women stop the wedding—and why Ashe wants to remind us that despite our fears of letting others down, it’s always better to walk away.

“I realized that the money spent and the embarrassment of canceling a wedding were nothing compared to the cost of marrying someone who had betrayed my trust,” Smith says. “I’d much rather have a wedding dress I never wore than enter a marriage that was false.”

Women who call off a wedding usually think that facing their family and friends will be the most difficult part. But Smith, Woller, and Williams all agree that their loved ones met them with grace and understanding. Woller even says that confiding in her friends and family led to closer connections and more vulnerability.

“I think the hardest part was accepting that the future I’d imagined wasn’t going to happen,” Smith says. “I think people underestimate how much emotion goes into that. You picture the day, the people who will be there, the life you’ll build afterward, and even the family you might have together.”

And despite knowing that the relationship is wrong or that calling the wedding off is the right decision, it doesn’t make ending the relationship any easier. “Even when you’re certain, you’re still human,” Williams says. “I spent the first few days sad, wondering if I’d walked away from my one chance at a fairy tale.”

In the part of the audio that doesn’t usually make it to the TikTok videos, Ashe sings, “This isn’t cold feet, these aren’t fireworks. The sky is menacing and that’s an iceberg.”

But nerves and wedding day jitters are always advertised to us as a normal part of your big day. How do you tell when it’s something more?

“Cold feet is usually about the commitment itself: the finality, the change in identity, the idea of ‘forever,’” Kavanagh explains. “A bad gut feeling is about the person: how they treat you, whether you feel safe and respected, and patterns you’ve been explaining away.”

Psychologist Karen Stewart, PsyD, explains that anxiety alone isn’t typically proof that something is wrong in the relationship. Instead, if you feel like you can’t trust them, don’t feel emotionally safe with them, or have unresolved patterns of conflict, these are all typically signs that the relationship isn’t sturdy enough to support a marriage. She also emphasizes that foundational issues in a relationship will not magically improve after a wedding. In fact, they’ll typically only get worse.

Some people are just anxious, and it can be hard to trust their gut feelings. But when you analyze where your anxiety is coming from, you can typically pinpoint whether it’s just your body reacting to a huge life choice or something deeper about your relationship.

“Sometimes we know far more than we are willing to admit,” Woller says.

It always comes back to thinking about the connection and compatibility you have with your partner. “If you can picture yourself five years from now, crying in the bathroom at 3 a.m., wondering why the hell you married this person, there’s your answer,” Williams says.

Calling a wedding off can be devastating. You’ve often spent months (or even years) planning a day, telling everyone you know, and picturing a future with this person that you love. Losing all of that at once can feel like something you’ll never get over.

But this is why Ashe sings over and over again that none of it matters. Everything is secondary to your happiness and emotional safety. And as you can see through the countless TikTok videos, deciding to call off a wedding can save your life.

“I genuinely believe it was both the worst and best thing that’s ever happened to me,” Smith says. Since calling off her wedding, she’s traveled extensively, made strides in her career, and learned more about herself than ever before.

Williams feels the same: “I’ve spent the years since investing in myself and my career, and I’ve learned that life doesn’t begin with a proposal or end with a canceled wedding.”

Although Woller regrets how she handled certain things in that season of her life—and is even sorry for the pain she caused her former partner—she’s learned to move on from the guilt and shame she felt in the aftermath of breaking her engagement.

“I still feel 100 percent confident that it was the right decision for both of us, and I am thankful every day that I dared to make such a life-altering choice.”

#6759. Michael J. Fox Shares the Best Advice His Father Ever Gave Him

“My parents were risk averse,” the 65-year-old Back to the Future actor says. “But I wanted to do all this crazy stuff.”

Every product was carefully curated by an Esquire editor. We may earn a commission from these links. Here’s how we test products and why you should trust us.

Michael J. Fox is a fighter. When the Back to the Future and Spin City actor was diagnosed with Parkinson’s disease in 1991, he refused to let the condition cut his life or his career short. He kept acting, earned some Emmy Awards and Golden Globes statuettes, and most recently appeared on season 3 of Apple TV’s Shrinking with Harrison Ford.

As the 65-year-old actor tells Esquire editor in chief Michael Sebastian in the latest episode of “What I’ve Learned,” his drive all stems from a quote his father told him when he left Canada to pursue acting in America: “Well, if you’re going to be a lumberjack, you might as well go to the goddamn forest.”

Though Fox’s father, a former Canadian Armed Forces soldier, was likely talking about Hollywood, Fox says that he took his advice to mean that if you’re going to do anything in life—whether it be his acting, his activism, or even his sheer optimism in the face of disease—you better fully engage yourself in it.

“My parents were born into the Depression ... so they were risk averse. Their whole life was just to have a nice life that didn’t leave anything exposed,” Fox continues. “And I wanted to do all this crazy stuff.” Namely Teen Wolf, three Back to the Future films, playing onstage with Coldplay, poking fun at his condition on Curb Your Enthusiasm (“because it’s hilarious”), and never letting Parkinson’s slow him down.

“I went through eight years of keeping it to myself and trying to forestall it with exercise and diet, learn a little bit from the doctors and experts, and then when I decided to share it it was like this great slap on the forehead,” Fox says about why he formed the Michael J. Fox Foundation. “I thought, There’s all these people that are experiencing this. Why aren’t we sharing our information about it?”

Elsewhere in the episode, Fox shares what he’s learned from Rush Limbaugh accusing him of faking his diagnosis, meeting Barack Obama before he was president, and raising roughly $3 billion for Parkinson’s research. You can watch the episode above, watch it on YouTube here, or listen to it on Spotify and Apple Podcasts.

“I don’t look at any time [in my life] and say, ‘Who was I then?’ or ‘Who will I be in a week?’ or “Who am I now?’ ” Fox says. “I just put my head down enough that I could still see where I was going and just plow ahead. And I realize now that I’m in this great position where I can have an effect on people’s lives.”

#6758. Pumpkin Cheesecake Muffins

Loaded with pumpkin seeds, canned pumpkin purée, and pumpkin pie spice, these big streusel-topped muffins are every fall lover’s dream come true. And to make things even better, the inside of each muffin is stuffed with a soft, lightly sweetened cream cheese mixture. Make the cheesecake filling and the streusel ahead of time to pace things out if it’s helpful. Don’t, even for a minute, consider using anything other than Libby’s 100% pure pumpkin purée (not pie mix). Nothing is better. We promise.

This recipe is part of Pumpkin Week 2026, Bon Appétit’s annual celebration of autumn’s most magical pantry staple.

Whisk ⅓ cup coarsely chopped raw pumpkin seeds, ¼ cup (31 g) all-purpose flour, 2 Tbsp. light brown sugar, ½ tsp. pumpkin pie spice, and a pinch of kosher salt in a small bowl to combine. Using your fingers, rub in 2 Tbsp. unsalted butter, room temperature, until moist pea-size lumps form. Chill until ready to use.

Do Ahead: Streusel can be made 1 day ahead. Cover and keep chilled.

Vigorously whisk 6 oz. cream cheese, room temperature, ¼ cup (50 g) granulated sugar, 2 tsp. all-purpose flour, 2 tsp. vanilla extract, and ½ tsp. Diamond Crystal or ¼ tsp. Morton kosher salt in a medium bowl until combined and smooth.

Do Ahead: Filling can be made 1 day ahead. Cover and chill.

Place a rack in middle of oven; preheat to 375°. Line cups of a standard 12-cup muffin pan with paper liners. Whisk 2 large eggs, 1½ cups (packed; 300 g) light brown sugar, 1 cup canned pumpkin purée, ¼ cup extra-virgin olive oil, 4 Tbsp. unsalted butter, melted, slightly cooled, ¼ cup whole milk, 1 Tbsp. pumpkin pie spice, 1 Tbsp. vanilla bean paste or vanilla extract, and 1½ tsp. Diamond Crystal or 1 tsp. Morton kosher salt in a large bowl until smooth, about 1 minute. Whisk 1¾ cups (219 g) all-purpose flour and 1½ tsp. baking powder in a small bowl to combine. Add to pumpkin mixture and fold in with a rubber spatula until just incorporated and no large dry streaks of flour remain (don’t overmix; batter will be lumpy).

Bake muffins until streusel is crisp and muffins spring back when gently pressed, 24–27 minutes. Let muffins cool in pan 5 minutes, then turn out onto a wire rack and let cool until just warm or completely.

Do Ahead: Muffins can be made 2 days ahead. Store airtight at room temperature.

#6757. ‘TMZ’ Confirms That [SPOILER] From ‘Love Is Blind’ Season 11 Got Divorced in June

The paperwork reveals exactly when Love Is Blind was filmed.

Only one couple from Love Is Blind Season 11 ended up saying “I do” this season, and it looks like they’re already done. Sarah and John got married in front of their friends and family in the show’s finale, but TMZ just confirmed that—according to Massachusetts court documents—Sarah filed for divorce on June 4.

The official reason? “An irretrievable breakdown of their marriage.” The docs go on to confirm that Sarah asked to split their finances, go back to her former name, and asked a judge “to bar John from restricting her freedom.”

The paperwork also gives us an exact filming date for Love Is Blind amid fan speculation that the show was in production well over a year ago. And...yep! Turns out Sarah and John got married all the way back on March 27, 2025, and then moved into separate homes a little over a year later on April 15, 2026.

So, what went wrong in their relationship? Fans saw them disagree on several major political issues throughout the season, but according to spoilers from Reality Receipts podcaster Rikkii Wise (@storytimewithrikkii), John had an “emotional affair” with an escort during the marriage. Truly yikes.

The exes will presumably get into the specifics during the Love Is Blind reunion, which was reportedly filmed a few weeks ago and airs this weekend on Netflix. The episode technically drops on Sunday, but Netflix is hosting a viewing event on Saturday with a live chat for those of you who want the tea early. Details below.

#6756. Novelty Tights Are the Easiest Way to Make Your Fall Outfits More Interesting

From polka dots to leopard print, these outfit ideas make simple fall looks feel fresh.

Every item on this page was chosen by an ELLE editor. We may earn commission on some of the items you choose to buy.

If you’re wondering how to get a little more creative with your outfits as the weather cools, look no further than novelty tights. From shimmery to polka dot, leopard print to lace, patterned tights instantly elevate even the simplest look, turning a basic skirt or dress into something with real personality.

The best part is how little effort it takes. A single pair can transform pieces you already own, giving them new life with no shopping spree required. Pair bold prints with understated layers, like a neutral sweater dress or a tailored blazer, so the tights take center stage. Or instead, give your look a more-is-more persona with extra sparkle or shimmer.

If you prefer to ease into the trend, sheer and lace styles offer a subtler way in, adding texture and a hint of drama without overwhelming an outfit. Whether you’re heading to the office, a dinner out, or a casual weekend errand, there’s a pair that fits the mood.

Ahead, we’ve rounded up some inspiration for how to style novelty tights this fall.

This holiday season, give your trusty LBD a fresh perspective with a pair of polka dot tights. We love a sheer burgundy style, which adds a rich pop of color that stands out against black without overpowering it.

Souliers Martinez Casilda leather platform Mary Jane pumps

Tory Burch Bunny Knot Small Leather Shoulder Bag

White or cream lace tights are underrated, if you ask us. We love how they look against a darker palette, such as a navy satin skirt and a matching cozy knit. The contrast feels soft and a little vintage, and the delicate pattern adds texture without stealing focus from the rest of the outfit.

Good American Good American Satin Bodycon Mini Skirt

Sometimes a mix of clashing prints and bright colors just makes sense. Here, a patterned trench coat and black polka dot tights come together with white sling-backs and a bright pink bag, proving that more really can be more when the pieces share a playful spirit.

Cing à Sept Almina Embellished Embroidered Trench Coat

A little (or maybe a lot) of extra sparkle is always welcome, and this all-black outfit gets a glamorous twist with intentional hints of metallics and shimmer, including the embellished tights. It’s the perfect look for date night, dinner with friends, or even a holiday get-together.

Cult Gaia HERA NANO RHINESTONE SHOULDER BAG

Many of us can agree that leopard is a neutral, and that goes for tights too. This ‘fit plays up the wild print and even adds a pop of periwinkle blue.

Susanna Chow Dorothy Leopard Leather Shearling Coat

If you’re already on the hunt for an outfit to close out 2026, a sparkly embellished dress and silver shimmer tights make the perfect combination. Complete your look with even more sparkly accessories and black velvet platforms.

Gianvito Rossi Sabrina velvet platform sandals

#6755. Source Speaks on ‘SLOMW’ Stars Jen Affleck and Zac Affleck Navigating Divorce and 4th Pregnancy at the Same Time

Jen announced her pregnancy just a few months ago.

Jen Affleck just filed for divorce from her husband Zac Affleck after seven years of marriage, news that comes just four months after the couple announced that they’re expecting their fourth child together. So, how are they planning to navigate a breakup and a pregnancy at the same time?

A source tells People that Zac “is doing okay” and that “He and Jen are staying focused on preparing for the baby and supporting their kids.” Judging from Instagram Stories, the pair seemingly celebrated their oldest daughter’s birthday together yesterday—the same day news broke.

The Secret Lives of Mormon Wives stars have a 5-year-old, 3-year-old, and 1-year-old, and announced the incoming arrival of their baby back in June:

Zac and Jen’s marriage problems have been chronicled on The Secret Lives of Mormon Wives, but Jen recently praised her ex while talking with People about the tough early months of her pregnancy. “I could not be there for my kids, and so Zac really had to show up. I mean, he didn’t really have a choice,” Jen said, before alluding to them having some issues. “There’s a lot that we’ve been navigating behind closed doors, but I do have to say, I wouldn’t have survived the first trimester without him.”

Reminder: Jen and Zac are appearing on the upcoming The Secret Lives of Mormon Wives: Orange County, so presumably we’ll find out specifics on what caused their split soon.

#6754. Piece Together the Perfect Pandora Holiday Gift for Her

From a little sparkle to a personal engraving, these pieces make a point of showing you care.

Great gifts prove you’ve been paying attention. They show you know what brings her joy, what she reaches for every day, and what she’d never think to buy herself. For the women who matter most in your life, that kind of thoughtfulness goes a long way. Mom, for example, deserves something that thanks her for a lifetime of being your first call when something goes wrong. Your sister craves a piece that feels unmistakably her. Your significant other needs something that says more than a dozen roses ever could. The trick is finding a gift with a little staying power, something sentimental, meaningful, and made to last. Jewelry does all three.

This is where Pandora comes in. Founded in Copenhagen in 1982, the jewelry brand has grown from a small Danish shop into a global name, known for its hand-finished designs and signature charm bracelets.

To make the search easier, we’ve picked five pieces for five important women: your mom, sister, significant other, best friend, and daughter. For each one, these sparkling options will become part of her daily rotation, a marker of a particular milestone, and a reminder that the person who gave it to her got it right. Consider this your holiday cheat sheet.

Your sister has always had a way of stealing the spotlight. Might as well give her the earrings to match. The set pairs sterling silver hoops with baguette-cut stones and detachable star charms, so she can switch up her look depending on the occasion. This means she gets two ways to wear them, because even after a lifetime of knowing her, you’re always discovering another side to her that’s worth celebrating.

Some things are better together, including the two of you. This pendant necklace brings three connected stars to life in sterling silver and 14k gold plating, giving the mixed-metal design a little extra dimension. It’s a thoughtful way to celebrate the person who makes your world brighter, shares your best adventures, and puts up with your quirks. The last part alone deserves a gift.

She may be growing up, but she’ll always be your little princess. This ring gives that title a stylish upgrade, with a golden chevron silhouette and a tapered baguette-cut stone at its center. Crafted in 14k gold plating, it’s a sweet reminder that she shouldn’t have to wait for a royal wedding to wear a tiara.

A good best-friend gift proves you know her taste almost as well as she does. The Sparkling Baguette Tennis Bracelet makes a strong case for that, with a sleek row of baguette-cut stones set in 14k gold plating. It’s polished enough to dress up a special occasion outfit, easy enough for every day, and just the sort of thing she’d pick out herself. Knowing her that well? That’s the real flex.

This tag pendant in sterling silver can be engraved with a date, a few meaningful words, or initials that form a message that needs no explanation to anyone else. She can wear it on its own or layer it with her favorite necklaces. The best part? You get to decide what it says. Mom has spent years remembering the little things about you. Now it’s your turn to give her something personal.
